#8368. Here’s Why You Shouldn’t Be Afraid to Harvest Does During the Rut

Published Nov 7, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

If there’s anything deer hunters can agree on, it’s that we love to argue. There’s plenty to debate: scent control vs. hunting the wind, treestand vs. saddle hunting, compounds vs. crossbows — the list goes on. Unsurprisingly, the advisability of harvesting does during the rut is no different.

Like many hunters this season, I’ve been patiently waiting for any mature deer to slip up during the craziness of the rut. Because, alongside the lone Pennsylvania buck tag in my pocket, are plenty of doe tags. A couple days ago, after passing a few doe fawns and young bucks in hopes that a more mature doe or buck walked through, I began to wonder: How many hunters are purposefully passing does during the rut? And more importantly, is it a good idea to wait or shoot? That’s why I reached out to Lindsay Thomas Jr., chief communications officer of the National Deer Association, for advice.

There are two lines of reasoning on why hunters should or should not shoot does during the rut. The more does running around, one argument goes, the more bucks those does will attract. That camp passes does during the rut because a live doe might walk by and bring a big buck in on her trail, or to the area you’re hunting when she goes into estrus in a few days.

I fall into the other camp. I agree that nothing beats a real doe’s scent — which is why I want to put a doe down nearby. Thomas agrees.

“My personal belief is there’s no better attractant for bucks during the rut than a doe on the ground in your hunting area,” says Thomas. “There is no better attractant. I don’t care what’s in a bottle. I don’t care what calls you’re using, nothing beats it.”

Both Thomas and I have experienced just how successful dropping a doe near your stand during the rut can be. Last year I dropped a doe about 40 yards from my stand early into an afternoon rut hunt. About an hour later, two small bucks came in to check her out, both presenting broadside shots multiple times. I chose to pass those two, but had they been bigger, more mature bucks, one wouldn’t have been so lucky.

“Many other hunters I know have seen this strategy work,” says Thomas. “Dr. Grant Woods has got some great video that he captured showing a buck actually using its antlers trying to get the dead doe to stand back up. By understanding tending behavior, we know that when a buck separates an estrus doe from the other deer, she will typically bed down until she’s ready to breed. Oftentimes this involves the bedded doe laying for upwards of an hour or two just chewing her cud until she’s ready. So, a doe lying on the ground smelling like an estrus doe and looking like an estrus doe is not at all alarming to a buck this time of year. In fact, it’s totally natural.”

If you’re interested in trying to fill an antlerless tag and shoot a buck during the same hunt, it’s important to do your best to put that doe down close to your setup. It’s not unusual for deer to run up to 100 yards after the shot (particularly if you’re bowhunting). In my mind, if a doe piles up that far away, it lowers the likelihood that a buck comes into bow range if he cruises by to check her out.

True, it may not matter because a buck could follow her scent on the exact same trail she took and present the same great shot. It’s also just as likely that another buck cruising through the area catches a whiff of her where she fell and beelines straight to her. If she’s lying dead out of sight, you may never see him. Personally, I get down, tag her, and bring her closer to my setup as quickly and quietly as possible. (Plus, in Pennsylvania, you have to tag the first deer you harvested before filling any other tags.)

Thomas is less inclined to climb out of his stand.

“I don’t think you want to get down and spend an hour blood trailing if it’s not going to be an easy blood trail,” says Thomas. “Walking all around that area is a ‘no’ for me, but if you saw her go down in some brush and know right where she is and you can make it quick, dragging her into the open where she’s visual from more areas might be a good thing for the remainder of your hunt.”

It’s worth noting that it is a hunter’s duty to always ethically dispatch and recover the first animal you shot, so make sure to pick your shots wisely, make smart choices about the recovery, and, if you’re choosing to use the doe as an attractant, only do so on cold days where the meat won’t spoil while you sit for a few more hours.

Regardless of your opinions on rut tactics, the NDA has been a proponent of harvesting does as early in the season as possible to meet your properties management goals. Experts say shooting whitetail does during the rut can also support healthy deer herds. Since one adult doe eats 6 to 10 pounds of forage per day, managing the overall deer population becomes more crucial as winter approaches.

“There are three things that help produce good bucks: age, nutrition, and genetics. We can only control two of those, age and nutrition,” says Thomas. “And by taking a doe as early as possible, or even during the rut, you’re helping enhance nutrition. You’re bringing the overall deer density down [and] producing more forage for other deer to help get them through the winter.”

Read Next: Quit Worrying About Genetics: How Successful Deer Hunters Think About Antler Growth, Cull Bucks, and Passing Good Deer

In short, shooting does early in the season leaves more forage for the rest of the herd, enhancing the health of your overall herd, and enhancing the nutritional output of the habitat for your bucks, resulting in larger, healthier bucks next season. It’s also more difficult to fill doe tags the later you wait into the season as does get spookier and harder to pattern during the late season due to hunting pressure and food availability.

As we enter mid-November, many deer hunters across America are reaching the top of the bell curve of rut activity. In some areas, we may already be on the backside of peak rut. If you’re still holding your buck tag and a few doe tags, get out and hunt. If a big doe piques your interest the next time you’re hunting, you can rest assured that putting your tag on her during the rut is only helping your odds that a big buck might just come into your area to check her out.

Derek Horner is the manager of audience development for Outdoor Life where he manages OL’s social accounts and email campaigns, and shares our stories with the world. He was born and raised in central Pennsylvania where his father and grandfather taught him to hunt and fish. He graduated from Pennsylvania State University in 2018 and still lives in central Pennsylvania.

#8365. This Is Why Your Trail Cameras Aren’t Working Properly

Published Nov 7, 2026 1:00 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

You’ve finally got your trail camera set up in the spot. Every day or two it sends you a photo of a giant old buck, and now all you’ve got to do is wait for the right wind to sneak in there and hunt him. And then, all of a sudden, the camera goes dead.

It’s a new camera, the battery life seemed fine, cell reception was decent … What the hell? Welcome to the often frustrating world of modern trail camera use.

I’ve been field testing and running trail cameras year-round for several years now and in my experience, there is no camera brand out there that’s immune to malfunctions and failures. But my experience pales in comparison to that of Rich Howell of Trail Cam Pro. Howell has been objectively testing and experimenting with trail cameras for more than 20 years.

I recently interviewed Howell on the Outdoor Life podcast and he shared several reasons why hunters see so many camera failures in the field. Some of it has to do with how we’re using the cameras, and some of it has to do with how the cams are made (which is usually pretty cheaply). Plus, Howell hints at where he thinks the future of trail cameras is headed, and it’s all about making a system that is more affordable and useful to hunters. It’s a conversation worth listening to ahead of the heart of hunting season.

In the meantime, here’s why our cameras often go down in the field, and what we can do to fix them.

Standard alkaline batteries often don’t have the required power to keep a trail camera running reliably, Howell says. If you’re getting fine daytime pictures, but blank, black images at night, it’s a good sign you have a battery issue as there’s not enough power for the camera to fire the flash. This can happen even when the camera is displaying decent battery life.

More expensive lithium batteries can work well, but they too can have issues. Howell noticed that about 1 in 20 lithium batteries would go bad very quickly, (like within days of use) and sometimes this was enough to prevent the camera from working.

Howell now suggests that hunters use rechargeable lithium batteries, which have proven to be more reliable. Having a pool of these batteries on rotation will help solve many trail camera power troubles.

When hunters first jump into the cellular trail camera game, they often like to get their photos sent immediately. In the beginning it’s fun to get a notification and then have that instant gratification of seeing a deer photo.

But it’s usually more effective to have a cell camera batch send images every 12 hours or 24 hours. This is a key way to preserve battery life, and it’s also a more useful way to analyze trail camera data.

Personally, I like to have cameras send every 24 hours and then I review all the cameras everyday in the morning. Looking at all the cameras on the property at once helps me put together activity trends.

Howell says that solar panels definitely can help keep your camera powered, but they often have issues in the field. First, you want to make sure that they are facing south to capture as most sun as possible. This can be a challenge if you want your camera facing north. And second, squirrels have a tendency to chew through solar panel wires. If you’re setting up your camera and panel in the hardwoods, there’s a good chance a squirrel is going to get to it. In other words, solar panels can be a useful tool, but they’re not a silver bullet.

If you’re using the stock camera strap, you’re just asking for trouble. The tag ends of these straps always seem to come loose (triggering the camera) and you’re never able to position the camera perfectly on the tree with them. Plus, other hunters can easily spot the strap, which is an important consideration if you’re hunting public land. Howell suggests using an adjustable tree mount to make sure you can position the camera exactly where it needs to go. You can find a wealth of  trail camera information on the Trail Cam Pro website and by subscribing to their newsletter.

Alex Robinson is Outdoor Life’s editor-in-chief. He oversees an ace team of writers, photographers, and editors who are scattered across the continent and cover everything from backcountry sheep hunting to trail running.

#8364. A 3,300Km Walk Across Japan in Winter

Japanese publisher Katsunari Tanaka has set himself the challenge of hauling a handcart 3,345km from Japan’s northernmost tip to the southern edge of Okinawa in winter.

Tanaka, 49, set off from Cape Sōya in Hokkaido on October 7 and is heading south to Cape Hedo in Okinawa. He hopes to complete the traverse in 100 days over five months. As he will have to contend with some challenging winter conditions along the way, he has described it as “playing chase with winter.”

Rather than carrying everything on his back, he is pulling a handcart that weighs around 100kg. He also has a support team with a vehicle in case of emergencies. At some points, different members of his team have walked with him for a day or so. His daily target is around 35km, with rest days and organized stops built into the schedule.

The first few weeks have already brought strong headwinds, rain, and some steep climbs, making progress difficult at times. Temperatures have dropped close to freezing. At the end of October, he crossed a mountain pass between Yoichi and Iwanai. There, headwinds stirred up the gravel as he walked. Then the snow began. In the end, he took refuge with his support team until the weather improved.

Despite the conditions, Tanaka reached Hakodate Harbor on November 3, having covered 621km across Hokkaido. From Hakodate, he is crossing the Tsugaru Strait by ferry to Aomori, then will continue south along the Sanriku coast to Sendai.

From there, he follows the Pacific coast through Fukushima to Tokyo before joining the Tokaido route toward Osaka before heading west to Kyushu. A second ferry crossing brings him to Okinawa, where he will walk to his final destination, Cape Hedo.

Along a road in Hokkaido. Photo: Katsunari Tanaka/Instagram

This is not Tanaka’s first experience of traveling Japan with a handcart. Around a decade ago, he spent three years visiting all 47 prefectures, selling books directly to people he met along the way. Loading his publications onto a cart, he covered the country between 2013 and 2016, eventually selling 13,477 books. At the time, many people questioned what he was doing and whether books could really be sold on the street. But he says the experience shaped his approach to business and his belief in the power of personal connections.

Tanaka insists that he is not an adventurer. Instead, he is traveling the country as a “merchant.” This time, he is making the journey to support his mentor, Arthur Hollands, a Japanese evangelist known for undertaking lengthy walks while carrying a large wooden cross. Along the route, Tanaka plans to stop at 11 locations to give lectures. He welcomes people to join him for sections of the walk.

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

#8361. How To Store Your Backpacking Gear for Winter Without Ruining It

Winter doesn’t have to mean the end of your backpacking season, but many hikers do feel the need for a little break once temperatures dip below freezing, snow starts falling, and conditions become a bit more dangerous. If that’s you, make sure you store your gear properly for the winter, or you might get an unpleasant surprise next spring when it’s time to pack for your first hike of the year.

Sometimes, we don’t know that our last trip of the season will be the last trip when we get home from it; I’ve found myself putting off unpacking from a trip only to realize in the springtime that my gear has sat abandoned and gross all winter long in my basement. Let this article serve as your reminder: taking care of your gear is one of the easiest ways to extend its lifespan and one of the most underrated ways to keep your budget in check during the hiking season.

If your gear, like mine, is currently caked in mud and stashed in your gear room, spend some time this week getting things in order to wait out a winter.

If nothing else, take five seconds to pull your sleeping bag or quilt out from a compression sack.

Down insulation is an absolute marvel. It has an incredible warmth-to-weight ratio but, if you leave it squished, the clusters of down feathers will lose their loft and their ability to trap warm air. Instead, shake out your bag in a dry room, which will also help any lingering moisture or body oil to escape.

Your sleeping bag or quilt only works well if the down inside stays lofted. Photo via Katie Jackson

If it smells like traditional hiker stink, you can choose to give it a gentle wash in a front-loading washing machine using a technical soap like Nikwax Down Wash Direct. Follow this up with a long stint in a dryer on low heat with a few clean tennis balls to help break up and redistribute the clumps of down.

Once thoroughly dry, store it in a loose, large bag or hang it in a closet where the down can expand and breathe freely.

Your inflatable sleeping pad requires some attention before going into hibernation, too. Storing an inflatable pad with the valve tightly closed traps moisture and breath condensation inside the chambers, which creates a breeding ground for mold and mildew.

Each time you breathe into your pad to inflate it, you’re also trapping a bit of moisture in there. Photo via Katie Jackson

Inflate the pad fully, wipe down any dust or salt from your sweat with a damp cloth, and inspect it for slow leaks so you are not surprised when you pull it out next spring. Once it is completely clean and dry, store it loosely rolled or laid flat with the valve left wide open so air can circulate freely and prevent the internal adhesive from breaking down prematurely.

Never pack a tent away even slightly damp, or you will pull it out in March to find ugly black mildew spots eating through the fly and a stubborn, musty odor that is nearly impossible to remove. Trust me, I speak from experience.

Tents are far too expensive to buy a new one each year. Photo via Katie Jackson

Pitch the tent in your living room or backyard, sweep out all the accumulated dirt and dead bugs, and wipe down the floor and mesh with a damp rag. Store the tent body and fly loosely folded or stuffed inside a large breathable pillowcase rather than its tight stuff sack, and leave the tent poles collapsed without tension so the internal shock cords do not lose their elasticity.

You’re almost done, I promise. From your pack, shake out all the crumbs and dirt from the main compartment, empty every pocket, and scrub away mud spots with a toothbrush and soap.

Of everything in this article, your pack is among the easiest pieces of equipment to winterize. Photo via Katie Jackson

Let the entire harness dry completely in a well-ventilated room so mildew does not take root in the padded hip belts or shoulder straps. Once it is completely dry, store your pack hanging up or sitting loosely in a closet, making sure not to cram it into a suffocating space that will permanently warp the frame sheet or load lifters.

Electronics need a little preventative maintenance to survive the winter without leaking battery acid or losing their charge capacity. Pop open the battery compartments on your headlamp, satellite messenger, and portable power banks to remove alkaline or rechargeable lithium-ion cells if you plan to leave them sitting for months without use.

Even rechargeable batteries can degrade over time, especially without proper care. Photo via Katie Jackson

Store your power banks at roughly a fifty percent charge rather than completely empty or maxed out at 100 percent, which helps preserve long-term battery health over the cold months. Keep these electronics in a cool, dry place inside your house rather than a freezing garage where extreme temperature swings can ruin the battery.

Finally, we look at your kitchenware and footwear. For your hiking boots and trail runners, clean off the caked-on dirt with a stiff brush. Your shoes probably still have a decent chance of getting worn during the winter months, so there’s no need to completely pack these away unless you want to.

Be so honest with yourself: how many true, soapy washes did your dirty water bag get this season? It may be overdue … Photo via Katie Jackson

For your kitchen and water setup, empty your water bladders and bottles completely, wash them with warm soapy water, and prop the reservoirs wide open so air circulates inside. Most importantly, remember that hollow-fiber filters like the Sawyer Squeeze must never freeze. If residual water inside the fibers turns to ice, it will instantly expand and crack the internal membrane, making the filter completely useless. Be sure your filters are thoroughly dry, sanitize them according to the manufacturer’s instructions, and store them indoors where they will never face sub-freezing temperatures.

Hollow fiber filters that have been stored for winter sometimes lock up, so make sure you test yours before taking it on another trip in the spring; you may need to soak the filter in distilled water overnight, or even for multiple days, to rewet the fibers and get the filter flowing again.

This all may feel like a tedious chore, but being kind to your gear now is how you make sure your gear is kind to you in 2027. Of course, if you truly don’t want to pack up your gear for the season, check out our list of 11 snow-free trips you could take this winter instead!

The buyer pays the same price as they would otherwise, and your purchase helps to support The Trek's ongoing goal to serve you quality backpacking advice and information. Thanks for your support!

I'm Katie, a thru-hiker and ultrarunner based in Colorado. After thru-hiking the Colorado Trail, John Muir Trail, Continental Divide Trail, and Long Trail in back-to-back years, I'm looking forward to a little time of rest, relaxation, and hanging out with my cat. Or, who knows, maybe the AT. No one has ever accused me of being good at sitting still.

#8360. Hunting the ‘Old Ripper’ Was Almost More Adventure Than I Bargained For

Published Nov 7, 2026 4:00 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

I had just come back from the service in 1954. My wife was expecting our first baby, and I was running a general store on Highway 97 just outside of Bainbridge, Georgia. Hardly a day went by that someone didn’t come in and tell tales of Old Ripper.

Old Ripper was a large black wild hog that lived in the nearby Fowltown Swamp. That hog tore up more fences, broke more gates, broke the backs of more female hogs and was chased by more hunters than any critter there has ever been in this country. Ripper had been shot by several people. My Daddy fired at him seven times with an M-1 carbine and believed he hit him, but the hog never fell. He had been hunted with dogs and he had killed plenty of dogs. There never was a hog hound that could hold him. The last time the boar was run with hounds, he killed two of the best hounds in the county with his slashing tusks, so no one else ever put their dogs on Old Ripper.

Old Ripper got his name because there seemed to be absolutely nothing he couldn’t rip up or utterly destroy. Farmers around Bainbridge went to the hardware stores and bought brand-new aluminum gates to put in the gaps he made in their fences. Within a week, the gates were often all bent and torn up where that crazy boar had stuck his nose under and bent them to get into a pasture or field. There was no hog wire or barbed wire fence that could keep Old Ripper out.

That boar was walking destruction. The farmers hated him not only for tearing up fences and gates, but even more because of what he did to their sows. Ripper would break into a pig parlor, tear up the pens and release all the sows. Then he would take them off for a stroll in the woods. Old Ripper weighed 438 pounds. So when he mounted some of the young sows, he broke their backs. Then the sows would have to be destroyed. The sows he did breed threw shoats that were as wild and crazy as he was. The piglets wouldn’t fatten and brought almost nothing at the market.

Everybody was out to get Old Ripper. My dad was accustomed to catching wild hogs. We used to catch them in the Fowltown Swamp, fatten them and sell them at the market. My daddy knew about trapping hogs. He built traps to catch Old Ripper. On two separate occasions Old Ripper did go into one of daddy’s traps, but he tore up the wooden stockades and broke out both times. My daddy wouldn’t stand for that, so daddy got a steel bear trap and set it for Ripper. When daddy went back the next day he found the giant tracks of Old Ripper and followed them to the trap. But instead of catching a renegade hog, he found the bear trap had been sprung by the big boar.

One reason Old Ripper became a legend was his ability to outthink the farmers and the hunters in our area. He had been around humans so much that in many cases he just outthought folks instead of using brute force. The people around our place began to think Old Ripper might be a supernatural creature. He was a boar, but he was a smart boar. No dogs could hold him, no stockades could enclose him, no fence could stop him and no man had been able to kill him for seven years.

Other episodes added to the legend of Old Ripper. One of the meanest things Old Ripper ever did involved Mrs. Ella T. Hand’s daughter-in-law, who was five months pregnant. She was in her kitchen and heard a disturbance out among her sows. She went out to the pig parlor and saw Old Ripper trying to tear the planks off the posts where she had her sows penned up. She picked up a rock and threw it at the old boar to try and run him off. When he saw her, he turned, looked straight at her and charged. Only a few steps away was a giant red oak stump that stood about three feet off the ground. The lady climbed up on the stump and began to scream for her husband. All the while, Old Ripper was going around the stump, snorting and blowing. He attempted to rear up on that stump and get to her but she stayed just out of reach of his razor-sharp tusks. For an hour the hog circled the stump as the lady screamed for help. Finally her husband came home. When he saw what was happening, he grabbed his shotgun from over the fireplace and ran toward the hog. At less than 10 yards, he shot Old Ripper in the side of the head with birdshot. The hog merely shook his head, looked at the man and trotted off.

To say Old Ripper was hated by everybody in the community would be a vast understatement. Every man and boy dreamed of killing that old boar, and plenty of work was forgotten by the men who hunted him. All it took to organize a community hog hunt was for someone to report that they’d seen Old Ripper. Every man and boy within earshot would grab a gun and head for the swamp.

I would have liked to have hunted Old Ripper, but I had mouths to feed. I didn’t have time to waste on that renegade boar. I had just bought a brand-new lever-action Marlin .30/30. I was proud of that gun and took it out behind the store to test it out. On that particular day, Mrs. Ella T. Hand happened to be sitting out in front of the store. Mrs. Hand was close to 80 years old. She always wore a sunbonnet, a long dress and about 90 percent of the time, she had a dip of snuff in her mouth. Mrs. Hand saw me kill a crow sitting in the field about 150 yards away with that rifle. I knew I’d made a lucky shot, but she didn’t. She told everyone for a month about how good I could shoot with that new .30/30.

“If that old wild hog comes back around my sows, I’m going to come and get you, Jack, and let you kill him with that new rifle,” she told me.

Well, about a month later, here comes Mrs. Ella T. in her 1949 pickup truck.

“Jack, that old boar is up in my field with some sows he run off,” she told me. “Now I don’t expect you’ll have any better luck than anybody else who has tried to kill him, but I sure wish you’d bring that new rifle up to the field and give it a try.”

I closed up my little country store and got into the front seat of that pickup truck with Mrs. Ella T. to try my hand at Old Ripper. I had five cartridges with copper-jacketed lead-headed bullets in my gun—the kind that open up but still go on through. There wasn’t any doubt in my mind that if I could hit that boar, the bullets would do the damage needed to put him down.

We drove to Mrs. Hand’s 40-acre peanut field, which backed up to the edge of Fowltown Swamp. I saw Old Ripper out in the middle of the field with four sows he had just broken out from their pen. The five pigs were all rooting in the peanuts. There was a hog wire fence around the field, with barbed wire on the top. The field, which was about 250 yards wide and about twice as long, was on a hillside. Old Ripper and the sows were up close to the top of the hill and I was down near the bottom. There was a dried-up creekbed that ran under the fence. From the tracks in the wash, I figured this was where Old Ripper and the sows had gotten into the field. The creekbed was only about two or three feet deep, but it was big enough for me to crawl under the fence and still stay out of sight. I could lay down in the bottom of the wash, crawl along on my stomach and Old Ripper wouldn’t be able to see me.

Until that day, I had never seen Old Ripper. I had looked at fences he had torn up and I had seen mud on the sides of the trees where he had rubbed. I knew he was a big boar, but I never realized just how big. When I saw Old Ripper standing there in the sun, his black bristles gathered in the rays of light and made him look a deep royal blue. He was a magnificent animal standing about four feet high, and he completely dwarfed the sows following him.

I said to myself, “Doggone your hide, Old Ripper! I’m going to try and get a little closer to you.”

The wash meandered from the bottom of the slope where I was uphill to where Old Ripper was rooting in the peanut patch. Lying flat on my belly, I would crawl for 10 yards and then peep over the edge to see where Old Ripper was. Each time I looked, the old boar was about 10 yards farther from the place than he had been before. It was just like he had a sixth sense that warned him of danger. He kept the same distance away, about 230 yards from me, from the time I first saw him until I decided to shoot. After I had crawled 30 yards, Old Ripper and his band of runaway sows were within 10 yards of a thick broom sedge field. I knew that if he got into that field, I wouldn’t be able to see to shoot him.

The wash was shallower and just barely kept me out of the boar’s view. The shot had to be over 200 yards. So I laid the rifle down on the lip of the wash, looked through the open sights and aimed at the top of Old Ripper’s shoulder. When I squeezed off the first shot, I saw the dust fly off the boar’s shoulder and heard the bullet make a dull thud when it hit him, but Old Ripper showed no sign of being wounded.

Then I stood up. Old Ripper reared up on his hind legs and bellowed like a wild bull. The hair on the back of my neck stood up. I had caught or killed more than 100 hogs out of that swamp, but I had never heard a hog make a sound like that.

When Old Ripper came down on his front feet, he was on a dead run, headed right down that hill. I knew that when he got to the bottom of the hill, he had business with me. I should have killed Old Ripper with that first shot, but he was far from dead. I didn’t panic. I knew that before the boar got to me, I would have time to fire four more of those .30/30 shells. I thought there was no way I wouldn’t kill him before he got to me. I had faced other wild hogs before.

With every shot, I believed I was pumping more lead into Old Ripper, but he never slowed. He still came on toward me. I took my time, aimed, squeezed, heard the report of the rifle, jacked another shell into the chamber and fired again. I knew I was hitting the boar, but he never missed a stride.

When Old Ripper was less than 50 yards away, I heard the hammer fall on an empty chamber. That was the loudest, most desperate sound I ever heard in my life.

I looked around and saw one lone cherry tree with no limbs on it about 30 yards to my right, but my feet wouldn’t work. I knew I couldn’t stay where I was, but I couldn’t go anywhere else. Old Ripper just kept on coming. I finally threw that gun down and began to run. Then I saw Old Ripper, now 20 yards away, running on two crippled front legs. I knew then that I had a chance to outrun him, but just as that idea went through my mind, the big boar fell and rolled to a stop about 10 yards away from me. He never moved, he never grunted and he never made another sound.

There was a slight breeze coming from that dead boar to me. Old Ripper’s odor was the strongest, rankest smell I ever have smelled. It was awful. I waited awhile to make sure Old Ripper was dead. Then I looked to see where my bullets had hit. The first had entered the shoulder. The second went through the hog’s loin and had gone into his stomach. Two other shots had hit his fore shoulders and entered the chest cavity. One bullet just flat-out missed.

Looking at Old Ripper, I was kind of sad that he was gone. Even if he was a renegade, he was a magnificent animal. There had never been a boar like him before that I ever heard tell of, and there never was another.

Mrs. Ella T.’s son James and her husband Ressie helped me load Old Ripper onto the pickup truck. We took the boar to Mr. Smoke Roger’s platform scales at Fayetteville, Georgia. The boar tipped the scales at 438 pounds. We struggled to reload the boar and then drove back to Bainbridge. Dr. Cooper, the veterinarian at that time, examined him. Below Ripper’s inch-thick hide was a sheath of gristle that extended from his huge shoulders to the middle of his back. This sheath had been Old Ripper’s armor and had turned the shotgun pellets and rifle bullets over the years. Dr. Cooper said the boar was between 8 and 10 years old and was probably a throwback to some of the European wild boars let loose in this country years before.

Read Next: I’ve Been Charged by Bears and Moose. Wild Hogs Are Much, Much Worse

When my daddy saw that the boar filled up the bed of that pickup truck, he could hardly believe his eyes. He had wanted to be the one who killed Old Ripper, but since he didn’t, he was glad it was me.

“That ol’ hog has cost farmers in this area thousands of dollars and many good sows, as well as being responsible for a lot of little pigs that weren’t fit to sell,” my daddy said. “I’m glad he’s gone.”

Since then the old Fowltown Swamp where Old Ripper used to roam and where wild pigs lived and bred for hundreds of years has vanished. Old Ripper’s haunts are covered by Lake Seminole today, but I remember it like it was when that boar was less than 30 yards away on a dead run bringing hurt to me, and I heard the sound of the hammer on my .30/30 falling on an empty chamber.

#8359. First Ski Descent of Meru Central

Tiphaine Duperier and Boris Langenstein of France have notched another Himalayan first ski descent, this time down the southwest face of Meru Central (6,310m) in India’s Garhwal Himalaya. The skiers are still surprised by how lucky they were.

Duperier admitted they started the expedition with low expectations after several projects earlier in the fall all failed.

“Snow conditions had been unforgiving,” she wrote. “We figured there was only a 3-in-10 chance of skiing the southwest face of Meru.”

But when they got there, the mountain was, they added, “generous” with them.

The three-peaked Meru massif, located in the Gangotri Glacier region of the Garhwal Himalaya, lies between Shivling and Thalay Sagar. It has three peaks: 6,660m Meru South, 6,310m Meru Central, and 6,450m Meru North. The ski descent line is in purple. Photo: Duperier/Langenstein

“We spent six days of reconnaissance and three [days] climbing the peak, [thanks to] an unprecedented stroke of luck in finding a way through the icefall and perfect snow in the upper section,” she explained.

The icefall on Meru Central. Photo: Boris Langenstein

The icefall at the base of the peak was indeed one of the most complex passages. The pair climbed a 15m pitch of WI4 and left a safety rope, which they also used to rappel down after the ski descent, Duperier told PlanetMountain.

Otherwise, the pair of regular expedition and skiing partners climbed alpine-style and pretty fast, reaching the summit in less than eight hours. According to their research, they completed the second-ever ascent of the southwest face of Meru, after an Indian team in 2023.

At the summit on Meru Central. Photo: Duperier/Langenstein

The skiers estimate they covered 1,500m on their descent, with some sections reaching 50 °. Snow conditions were good overall, hard but with a good grip.

“We skied like little monkeys on the wires in Rishikesh,” Duperier posted on social media. “It was just incredible, truly beautiful!”

While the climbers rappelled down the icefall onto the glacier below, the ski descent itself had no rappels or interruptions.

Duperier barely had time to return home to France to pick up a Piolet d’Or for the first alpine-style ascent of the Schell Route on the Rupal Face of Nanga Parbat, followed by the first ski descent from the 8,125m summit.

Tiphaine Duperier at the Piolets d’Or ceremony. Photo: Piotr Drozdz

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

#8356. This South Dakota Hunter Borrowed a Bow and Tagged a 197-Class Deer — His First Archery Buck

Published Nov 8, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

It wasn’t the best conditions for bowhunting in central South Dakota on November 3rd. It was misting and drizzling rain and the wind was blowing between 20 and 30 miles per hour.

But Colby Claussen had seen a huge buck the previous week out of a tree stand and decided to buy a ground blind and place it near the trail where the buck had walked.

“I spotted him a week earlier from my stand at nearly 70 yards walking a trail along a field edge near a standing corn field,” Claussen tells Outdoor Life. “He was working scrapes. I decided to buy an enclosed blind and put it in the woods 30 yards off the trail he walked.”

A week later he was in the blind hunting the buck he’d seen on trail cameras off and on for several years.

“I first saw him several years ago through binoculars while hunting,” Claussen says. “The buck was a good 10 pointer then and about 600 yards away – way too far to shoot.”

After that the 20-year-old hunter got trail camera pictures of the buck and watched him grow over several years.

“This year I saw him from my treestand working scrapes, but he was too far for me to try a shot with the bow I’d borrowed from a friend,” says Claussen, a just-graduated student who farms with his family. The giant buck would be his first ever taken with a bow.

By 1 p.m. on Nov. 3, Claussen was in his ground blind, protected from wind and rain. He spotted a couple small bucks and does that afternoon. Just 10 minutes before shooting light ended that day, he spotted the enormous whitetail he was after.

“He stepped out of the woods to work a scrape at 30 yards, but it was too thick to shoot, so I waited,” Claussen says. “The buck stayed on the trail and kept coming toward me until he got in an opening, broadside at 30 yards.”

Claussen drew his bow and shot an arrow tipped with a G5 Megameat 3-blade expandable broadhead. It hit the buck perfectly behind its shoulders, driving through both lungs, clipping its heart, and exiting the massive deer.

“I knew he was hit well, and he went to the treeline, walked in a small circle, and disappeared into the timber,” says Claussen, who owns Sodak Drone Application, an agricultural business. “I went to where he stood and found my arrow and plenty of blood.”

Claussen waited 30 minutes before trailing the deer, then quickly found the buck just 60 yards from where he’d shot it.

He called family members who soon arrived and helped with field dressing and loading the estimated 300-pound buck into a pickup truck. Then they took it to a meat processor, with the buck’s cape and head delivered to a taxidermist.

Read Next: The Best Hunting Blinds of 2026, Tested and Reviewed

A taxidermist measured Claussen’s buck at 198 1/8 inches, and another skilled deer rack scorer came up with 197 7/8 inches. The non-typical rack has nine points on one side, six on the other, with an inside spread of 21.25 inches. Antler base circumferences are 5 3/8 inches. The buck’s estimated age is 5.5 to 6.5 years.

“A couple years ago I had photos of that buck with half a broken rack from fighting,” Claussen says. “I quit hunting him when he broke his rack. But another hunter that year missed him in rifle season.

“I’m sure glad that hunter missed, and I got another chance to take my buck of a lifetime.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

#8355. Arctic Town Kills Dozens of Grizzlies Feeding on Dump

For years, the grizzlies near Inuvik in Canada’s western Arctic relied on a stable source of food — the town dump. Generations of bears grew up feeding there. In 2025, wildlife officials found that over 40 grizzlies regularly visited the dump.

In 2025, before the fence was built, trail cameras captured multiple grizzlies hanging around the dump, looking for food. Photo: Government of the Northwest Territories

It was too much. This year, the town built an electric fence around the site, hoping the bears would move on. They didn’t. Some started coming into town looking for food. Others just hung around the fence, searching for a way in.

“They would just do these laps around the dump, and they would just be emaciated, sickly,” said Toby Halle, a Renewable Resources officer based in Inuvik, told the CBC. “They don’t know what to do with themselves. And quite frankly, we don’t know what to do with them.”

Inuvik, population about 3,500, was the first community in that part of the Arctic to try to wean the bears off their dependence on dump food with such a fence, which cost about $700,000. The problem was, the bears had not learned to depend on any other source of food.

“We’ve probably had three to four generations of bears that have been using that landfill,” said local mayor Peter Clarkson. In the end, the town took a drastic solution — but one that was probably unavoidable in the circumstances: They shot 28 bears.

Tragic though this is, officials hope that within the next couple of years, the remaining bears in the area will move on and learn to eat more natural fare.

Many other Arctic towns still have open dumps where bears come to feed. Photo: Government of the Northwest Territories

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8354. Day 35: Samaín: Halloween in Galicia

Unable to sleep until my alarm, I was up and starting the long day ahead before 5:30. Triacastela had transformed overnight into a spooky Halloween town. Okay, not really, but the misty cloud that had sunk into the valley made me think so.

With a lot of kilometers on the agenda, I took the planned route I discussed with my brothers and walked into the forest.

There was a quiet stillness permeating throughout.

Most might’ve called it eerie, but because of the comfort I learned to find in the dark, I savored it.

Today was Halloween in the US, Día de Los Muertos in Mexico, and Samaín here in Galicia.

In many traditions around the world, this time of year was believed to be when the veil between worlds was the thinnest. Today, I planned to lean into that energy and see what happened.

The sounds in the trees around me set the feeling of the day.

Birds I’d never heard before cawed and crowed.

Water droplets of cloud condensation pitter-pattered on the trees.

As the sun came up, I descended into another valley of clouds.

It was a town. As I moseyed along the streets through the dense fog, I came upon an old brick wall with rocks adorned with quotes. The phrases were wise and gave me something to contemplate. While reading them, I felt something brush my leg. I looked down and saw a puppy, ribs poking out a bit, and shaking. I bent down to pet it, and it jumped up, as if asking to be picked up. And so I did.

After setting it down and walking a few steps further, I found the source of the quotes and the dog: Terra de Luz (Land of Light). It was an old blacksmith property now open for pilgrims to simply stop, rest, and reflect. They had a table of food, a permaculture garden, and signs offering yoga, meditation, and integration. It felt like a place I could spend my whole day, if I didn’t have the biggest day of walking ahead of me. For the five minutes I was there, though, I definitely felt a wave of peace.

Though the environment looked eerie, the energy I felt was light.

All different types of dogs approaching me for pets.

Giant slugs crawling their way along the trail.

Newly born calves staring at me with their tongues out.

A field full of giant pumpkins ready for harvest.

As I walked, I researched how the local traditions celebrated the holiday of Samaín.

With roots in Celtic tradition, Samaín marked the end of the harvest. Roasted chestnuts (magostos) were eaten. Pumpkins and gourds were carved. And whispers of lore included witches (meigas) and mischievous spirits (trasnos).

The theme of the holiday naturally continued as I caught up to my Italian brother. We picked apples from a tree along the trail and noticed the many flowers adorning the gravestones for the day of the saints.

When we got into the forest again, we came upon a magnificent tree that looked to be older than the country of Spain itself. Call me a tree hugger, but as I touched its branches and took in its magnitude, I could feel its wisdom.

For the next portion of the walk, I chose to do it alone. I finished the last chapters of The Pilgrimage as I strolled under the many chestnut trees.

When I finished this book the first time, I felt it had an anticlimactic ending. Now that I’ve walked the road he recounted, the ending of the book felt impactful, like I’d learned the same lessons that he concluded at the end of his journey.

My journey wasn’t over, and I knew I had more to glean, but I definitely knew I’d found what I had come looking for.

And with those thoughts, I entered the most pilgrim-concentrated part of the Camino. Many people who didn’t have the freedom to do the whole route only had to walk the last 100 kilometers to receive a Compostela. This stretch would be busy, and I did my best to mentally prepare.

Well, that didn’t go super great. Many new pilgrims I didn’t know, combined with not getting a lot of alone time, weighed on my mood.

I made it to the 100-kilometer mark from Santiago. It didn’t feel real. Didn’t I just start walking?

When I arrived in Portomarín, I was completely exhausted, both mentally and physically.

Then the brothers and I ended the night with a new tradition: Uno. We were all exhausted and delirious, though, so the fun didn’t last long.

Early to bed for another day closer to Santiago.

#8352. Are Tree Saddles Really Better Than Treestands for Hunting Whitetails?

Published Nov 8, 2026 12:01 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Early in my bowhunting career, I set up a ladder stand on top of a ridge that had oaks and plenty of deer sign. On opening morning, I watched a deer parade go down a trail that was 20 yards out of range. I sat in that stand several more times and never had a deer come within bow range. If I had a mobile stand, I could have easily adjusted my hunting strategy to move closer to the active trail.

Mobile hunting for whitetails is all about making aggressive moves and constantly hunting fresh spots. Mobile hunters carry everything they need for a hunt so they can set up on the fresh sign and easily adapt to the conditions. That’s why they need lightweight and easy to use hunting platforms.

That old faithful ladder stand just won’t cut it when you need to slide another 100 yards closer to a bedding area. A mobile setup is also great for public land where you can’t or don’t want to leave your stand. It’s also less expensive than buying multiple permanent stands.

There are three main choices for the mobile hunter: a climber, a hang-on stand, and a tree saddle. I’ve hunted from all three of these platforms in my search for the perfect mobile hunting set up. To help with this comparison, I also reached out to Aaron Warbritton, from The Hunting Public YouTube channel. He’s been at the tip of the spear of the mobile hunting trend and offers a wealth of experience. Together we’ll see how each platform stacks up against each other in five categories: weight, setup, huntability, comfort, and price.

A heavy pack can feel fine for a few minutes, but when yards turn to miles, you feel every pound. For hunting deep in public land, a lightweight set up is a must. Weights will differ depending on the model of stand and climbing method. These weights will give you an idea of what you can expect a complete kit to weigh. Here’s how all three systems compared:

Model: Tethrd Mantis Saddle and Predator platform.

Kit Includes: Saddle, tether, platform, lineman’s belt, and three climbing sticks.

Total Weight: 14.1 pounds. *The three climbing sticks separately weigh 7.5 pounds.

Kit Includes: Stand, safety harness, lineman’s belt, and three climbing sticks.

Total Weight: 23.9 pounds. *The three climbing sticks separately weigh 7.5 pounds.

If weight is your primary concern then a saddle is the clear winner. You can even make the saddle lighter by using a lighter climbing system.

Weight isn’t only a factor when carrying a stand, it also matters for setting it up. This is especially true when hanging a saddle platform or hang-on stand. When you climb to the height you want to hunt, you have to hold the stand or platform in one hand while tightening the stand’s straps with the other. A light platform for a saddle, like the Predator platform (3.3 pounds), is much easier to hang then the heavier base of a hang-on stand (12.2 pounds).

“I would say that now 90 percent of my hunts are with a saddle and a platform; just because it’s lighter, the footprint is smaller, and it’s faster for me to set up,” Warbritton says.

With a saddle and a hang-on, you’ll need your choice of hang-on stand and a climbing method. I use the Lone Wolf Alpha stand and climbing sticks. The stand and sticks were designed to pack neatly together making it a very convenient and easy to use system. You can climb the tree and hang your stand in one trip with practice and some paracord. I’m pretty slow and careful when climbing and it usually takes me 15 to 20 minutes to climb and get set. The hang-on is fast, but the saddle is slightly faster and easier for me to set up.

A climber is attached to the tree before you climb and doesn’t require climbing sticks. There’s also no platform to hang once you reach hunting height. You can scale a tree fairly quickly with one, and it’s a less technical climb than a saddle or platform. However, that speed comes at the cost of versatility. Climbers need a straight, limbless tree, which isn’t always available. I much prefer the versatility of other platforms that allow me to climb almost any tree. If you live in an area that has a lot of trees suitable for a climber, then these negatives might not be such a big deal.

The size of a climber is another negative. “I haven’t hunted out of climbers a ton, but I’ve hunted out of them enough to know I don’t want to use them. Their footprint is so big that when you’re packing them in, the stand gets tangled on stuff,” Warbritton says.

Another reason to move away from climbers is the noise they make when climbing. It’s discouraging to put a lot of effort slipping into your spot, but then make a scraping noise as you ascend into position. This is especially true when hunting close to bedded deer.

By comparison saddles and hang-ons are much stealthier to carry and set up. If you are proficient with either of them, you can be up a tree within earshot of a bedded buck without issue.

Proficiency doesn’t come overnight with any of these platforms, but tree saddles especially take time to learn. The saddle has a steeper learning curve because it’s so different than a traditional treestand. The first time you lean back into your saddle it might feel like leaning back on a rocking chair at the edge of a cliff. However, that uneasy feeling quickly fades and I personally feel much safer in my saddle than I ever felt in a hang-on or climbing treestand. That security comes from the fact that if you fall off your platform it is extremely easy to get back on because the tether is always pulled tight—making the drop almost unnoticeable.

Beyond the saddle’s learning curve, the hang-on and saddle are neck and neck when it comes to packing, setting up, and hunting out of them.

“They’re really close. They definitely have their differences. Whichever one that you decide to use, you need to practice with it. Whether you’re using climbing sticks or strap on steps, you need to practice packing, hanging, and then shooting out of them because they’re different,” Warbritton says.

When the rut kicks in and you basically live in a tree all weekend, a comfortable stand is a must. While all three mobile options are pretty comfortable, the standard treestand seat is hard to beat. Warbritton agrees with that preference.

“The only thing I like about a treestand is if I’m going to sit all day, I’d rather have a treestand,” he says.

Tree saddles are comfortable, but a lot of the comfort in a saddle depends on your tether height because it controls the weight distribution. You’ll need to find a sweet spot that works for you and that takes some practice. I haven’t found that sweet spot for comfort just yet, but I do think that I’ll eventually get the saddle to be as comfortable as my treestands.

Read Next: The Best Tree Stands, Tested and Reviewed

Like weight, the price will depend on the manufacturer and model. Just like our weight evaluation, we’ll compare the products I have personally owned and used.

A Tethrd mantis tree saddle, platform, lineman’s belt, tether, and a set of Lone Wolf climbing sticks will run you around $650. Without climbing sticks the system is about $500. If you want to add some accessories like Ropeman ascenders, pouches, and a recliner, you’ll be looking at spending around $900. It’s not a cheap investment, and you’ll have to decide if the above-stated benefits outweigh the cost.

Hang-ons and climbers are less expensive. A premium climber like the Summit Explorer SD and Hunter Safety System safety harness will run you around $450. A Lone Wolf Alpha hang-on, climbing sticks, Hunter Safety System safety harness, and lineman’s belt will set you back around $500. Again, prices will vary depending on what models, climbing methods, and add on accessories you choose.

Read Next: The Best Tree Saddles of 2026, Tested and Reviewed

Warbritton and I both prefer tree saddles because they are lightweight, easy to pack, fast to set up, and make mobile hunting easier. Is it the best system for you? You’ll have to evaluate the features against your hunting needs to make that decision. The good thing is, all three systems will give you the ability to be a mobile hunter.

“I just think that either way you’re going to be hunting mobile and you’re going to have a lightweight setup that’s going to allow you to see more country, learn more stuff, and potentially kill more deer,” Warbritton says.

Scott Einsmann is Outdoor Life’s gear editor. He oversees the gear team’s editors and writers who are subject matter experts in bows, knives, hunting, fishing, backpacking, and more. He lives in Richmond, Virginia with his wife and two bird dogs.

#8350. Leonard Clark: Explorer, Spy, Liar

Born in 1907, Leonard Francis Clark was an explorer, war hero, and writer who made daring expeditions all over the world before dying mysteriously in Venezuela in 1957.

Today, he isn’t well known, but has this lack of scrutiny been a blessing in disguise for this forgotten explorer’s legacy? His books, filled with adventure and excitement, read like fiction. The more one digs into the forgotten reports and old newspaper articles, the stranger his story, and the more complicated the man.

A Tibetan expedition under Leonard Clark in 1949. Photo: The Marching Wind

The main sources on Leonard Clark are all, well, Leonard Clark. This isn’t necessarily damning, but it is something he shares with most good con artists or cult leaders. The following is a short biography based only on official records, such as draft cards and census data. The first rule of reading historical accounts is that every historical figure is a liar.

Leonard Francis Clark was born in British Columbia, Canada, in January 1907 to two Americans, Maud and Francis Clark. In 1909, they moved back to Maud’s birth state of California, and Francis promptly died.

There are conflicting statements online about which university he attended, but census data shows him living only a few kilometers from the University of California (now the University of California, Berkeley) in 1930, so I’d put my money on that one. He began traveling right out of college, spending three years in Borneo on a hunting trip with his brother, Laurence. By the late 1930s, he was on a National Geographic-funded expedition to the South China Sea.

His article, cryptically titled Among the Big Knot Lois of Hainan, was published in the 1938 September edition. In it, he recounts how he and a small expeditionary force landed in the midst of a deadly cholera epidemic. Relying on hired porters to carry their supplies, Clark and Nicol Smith, another future travel writer, set off into the interior. Soon, malaria and infection laid low most of the team, including, per Clark’s article, Smith.

Again, according to Clark, Smith urged him to take the remaining five porters, the minimum of equipment, and keep going.

Much of the article is dedicated to ethnographic observations, with the typical exoticism and patronizing tone of the era. At one point, Clark noted that the Li people of Hainan reminded him of indigenous Amazonian people and “the men were tall, many six feet.”

A recent study showed that the average height for men in Hainan today is 168.7cm, around 5’6″, and the same study found that average heights had increased over the past century. This is a small nitpick, but he’ll repeat this unlikely claim again in the Amazon. It is worth noting that the “six-foot-tall Indian warrior” was a stock character in contemporary pulp adventure fiction.

There is an interesting contrast between Clark’s account and some of the newspaper reports, especially one from a Virginia paper, which relied on Smith for the story. This article implies Smith did much more than lay around his tent with malaria. Clark’s article claims he was the first white man to enter 47 different villages, while the newspaper says that Smith was the first Westerner to penetrate inland.

There is a third source: the 37-minute silent film compiled from the expedition’s footage and photographs. In it, we briefly see a white man lying under a mosquito net as if ill. But whether this is Smith, Clark — who admits himself to experiencing malaria symptoms on the expedition — or the third westerner on the expedition, one Jack Munroe, the film doesn’t say.

His previous experience in the region led him to return a few years later, this time on behalf of the U.S. Office of Strategic Services (OSS), a precursor to the CIA. His work during the war centered around the China-Burma-India corridor, particularly the Yellow River valley. He was involved in organizing guerrilla activities, building intelligence networks, and navigating the Japanese surrender of Taiwan.

In a later press statement, Clark explained how he had worked with General Dai Li, and that “the Reds had it in for him,” due to the infamously repressive actions Dai Li took against Chinese Communists. Dai Li, whom historian Barbara Tuchman called “China’s combination of Himmler and J. Edgar Hoover,” was killed in a 1946 plane crash. Wide speculation places the blame for this crash on either the Chinese Communist Party or the OSS. This would make things awkward for Clark a few years later.

He continued his government work in the area after the Second World War, sliding right into the developing Cold War. In 1949, Clark led a large, well-funded expedition into Tibet.

It would have gotten him in the history books if Leonard Clark identified a peak higher than Everest. Unfortunately for him, he didn’t. But he certainly proposed that he had, when he claimed that he’d surveyed Amne Machin and found it to be 9,040m high.

Amne Machin is not taller than Everest. Photo: Wikimedia Commons

Clark published his account of the expedition years later, titled The Marching Wind. In it, he wrote with his typical style: daring escapes, stereotypically aggressive natives, and breezy adventure writing. What it did not contain was the data to support his claims.

As soon as he released his first report and measurements, geographers, geologists, and mountaineers pored over them. Bradford Washburn, a cartographer, photographer, and explorer, published a thorough examination of the surveying techniques and questionable results from Clark’s expedition.

A contemporary review in the Alpine Journal, commenting on his photographic evidence, wrote that “anything more unconvincing could hardly be imagined — yet, ostensibly…the mountain was the object of the expedition.” More on that “ostensibly” later. Suffice it to say, Clark’s estimate that Amne Machin could be almost 1,000m taller than Everest was not adopted by geographers at large.

The review concluded that “it can hardly be wondered if mountaineers, whom Mr. Clark rather despises, cannot accept his results.”

Clark blithely remarked that he had “some rather bulky complaints by mountain and other specialists,” but admitted subpar instruments made his results “indecisive.”

A surveying party including Clark, far right, taking observations of Amne Machin. Photo: The Marching Wind

If he doesn’t seem too broken up, it may be because Amne Machin’s height wasn’t the main purpose of his trip. Clark was supported logistically and went on behalf of Ma Bufang, the fiercely anti-communist warlord ruling Qinghai, on the border of Tibet. Anticipating that he could not long hold out against the People’s Liberation Army, Clark proposed that his expedition would map a path of retreat. This would help Ma Bufang’s army cross into Tibet and continue their anti-Communist campaign from the mountains.

Clark envisioned Ma Bufang’s Qinghai, through Tibet, becoming “the bulwark against Red invasion of India.” Clark went on the expedition and did his mapping, but the PLA took Qinghai only months later, and Ma Bufang fled to Hong Kong.

In his introduction to his book, Clark claims that the United States was unaware of his “very slight aid” to Ma Bufang and local anti-Communist guerrilla activities, and he involved himself with the Chinese Nationalist army “as a private American citizen.” If I were to say that I was skeptical of this claim, I would not be the first to do so. But that falls under speculation, so I will say only that I sent a Freedom of Information Act request to the CIA regarding Clark’s work in Tibet. However, the records were not forthcoming.

After returning from the mountains, Clark went to a wedding anniversary celebration in Canton, surrounded by his fellow Allied intelligence members and their set. Things got a little out of hand, and Clark soon found himself in a Cantonese jail with a bullet wound and two manslaughter charges.

Chinese Nationalist soldiers on Clark’s Tibetan expedition. Photo: The Marching Wind

The other two men involved were Harold Harris, an Englishman who died immediately, and Willard Freeman from Massachusetts, who died later from his injuries. Clark also sustained a gunshot wound, but had recovered sufficiently by late November to be moved from hospital to prison.

The three had been drinking together in Freeman’s home when, at about 4 am, servants reported hearing gunshots. Mrs. Maya Freeman entered the room at 10 am to find an odd and grisly scene. Harris, fully clothed, lay dead on Freeman’s bed with three gunshot wounds. Freeman, completely nude, was also lying on the bed, alive with two shots to the abdomen. Clark, in his underwear, was on a second bed, with a single gunshot to the chest. Furniture was overturned, indicating a fight, and all three were covered in scratches. The only weapon found was Clark’s .32 caliber pistol.

Later that month, the Canton District Court denied Clark’s bail request. The court, still trying to determine what exactly happened, focused on the widowed Maya Freeman. Interviewing her for hours, they attempted to make her confess to an affair with Clark. Both of them repeatedly denied it. Clark further denied that he had fired any of the six shots, though he didn’t name the shooter.

Clark’s brother contacted former OSS head General William Donovan, who rallied the State Department. Clark reportedly was concerned that if he wasn’t released before the city fell to the Communists, he would be tortured for information regarding Dai Li and OSS activities. The State Department investigated and, thanks to its intervention, Clark was released.

Clark’s legal troubles were splashed across newspapers worldwide. Photo: The Journal (Caldwell, Ohio) November 24, 1949

What exactly happened in Freeman’s house that night? Was the dispute between the Allied Intelligence men personal, or professional? If the world were ever to find out, it wouldn’t be from Clark. Once freed, Clark returned to California and moved on to his next adventure. It was an ambitious one: Leonard Clark was going to discover the lost city of El Dorado.

He had an old parchment map to the lost city, he claimed, which showed it lay in the upper reaches of Peru. Never mind that it had been pretty well established for over a century that El Dorado was a myth, following the efforts of explorers Alexander von Humboldt and Robert Schomburgk.

Clark’s resulting account, The Rivers Ran East, contains all the genre tropes you would expect from a fictional work in the same setting. Native Amazonians are ferocious cannibals who capture Clark and keep him in a poison snake pit, crocodiles can and must be lassoed (there are no crocodiles in Peru, only caimans, a type of alligator), and there is a beautiful lady explorer, Inez Pokorny, who is secretly part of a London-based international El Dorado-seeking organization. If this organization existed, I have found no trace of it outside of this book.

If I were to list every blatant impossibility in his account, we would be here for days, so I’ll just continue on one particular thread: Leonard Clark seems to have a very loose grasp of which animals are in Peru and how big they are. At one point, he is blinded by a venom-spitting snake. There are three venom-spitting snake species, and none of them are in South America.

His 500-pound (227kg) boars must be peccaries, pig-like animals native to the Americas. The Amazon native white-lipped peccary tops out around 40kg, while the unconfirmed giant peccary still has only been estimated at 50kg. Likewise, his 12m-long pythons must be anacondas, which have a maximum recorded length of eight meters.

Clark claims to have found El Dorado. What he found was a group of Chicham people panning for gold, who traded some of it to Clark and Pokorny. According to his book, Clark traveled under the pretense of investigating botanical medicines, while actually searching for El Dorado.

In fact, as the El Dorado adventures on the page grew more and more ridiculous, I began to wonder if it were not the other way around. Here I have fully entered the realm of speculation. American corporations were, and still are, extremely interested in extracting resources from South America, including pharmaceuticals. In fact, Clark also reported success in this area, claiming to have identified over 60 native medicinals with commercial possibility — as well as Amazonian oil.

In praise of Clark’s work, Baron Wolfenegg, Director of Colonization for the Orient in Peru, accidentally said the quiet part out loud, stating he’d “learned much from this book that will help my work of colonizing this vast jungle.”

On Peru’s Perené River. Photo: The Rivers Ran East

It is practically gauche for explorers to die in their beds, and Clark, who seemed to mold his life to the shape of adventure stories, was no exception. Clark was in Venezuela in 1957, looking, he claimed, for diamond mines. He entered the Amazon basin, never to emerge again.

One history of the OSS offhandedly mentions that Clark died with “two native arrows in his back.” I suspect the explorer’s penchant for sensationalism survived his death. Other contemporary reports describe Clark as having drowned when his canoe overturned.

As I pointed out earlier, most of the information on Clark’s expeditions came from Clark himself. Since he never returned, startlingly little information is available about his death at age 49.

The area around the Orinoco River in Venezuela is, in fact, rich in diamond deposits. Photo: Wikimedia Commons

The line between fact and fiction is notoriously vague in much of the adventure travel genre. Is Herman Melville telling the full truth about his time spent on the island of Nuku Hiva? Did anyone actually complete the “Long Walk” out of a Soviet detention camp, which Slavomir Rawicz falsely claimed as his own tale? How much artistic license and embellishment is acceptable before exploration accounts become outright hoaxes?

I didn’t write this article with the goal of proving Leonard Clark a charlatan. It can hardly be denied that he really was going to these places, at the very least. But there are enough discrepancies and inaccuracies in his accounts to conclude that they should be taken with a grain of salt, and his treasure-hunting pseudo-archaeological tendencies make his anthropological findings, well, questionable.

As a contemporary New Yorker book critic put it, Clark had “the same gift, or weakness, for grandiose verbiage that got Marco Polo in trouble with his fellow Venetians.”

The real story, and one which may never be fully disentangled, lies in why, exactly, Clark was doing his exploring. Somewhere between the romantic adventurer and the cynically colonialist spy using exploration as a guise for the extractive, violent enforcement of Western capitalist hegemony, lies the truth.

“Every explorer has two faces,” Clark wrote in the opening to The Rivers Ran East; “the secret one and the one he shows to the world.”

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.
