#8337. Andrew Skurka on Why Experienced Backpackers Don’t Wear Hiking Boots

The backpacking equivalent of “happy wife, happy life” is “happy feet, happy hike.” So today, I’m going to help guide your decision-making when selecting footwear for your next backpacking trip.

Hi, I’m Andrew Skurka. I’m a former thru-hiker. I was the first person to do the Great Western Loop, the Alaska-Yukon Expedition, and the Sea-to-Sea Route.

If you’re watching this before Saturday, December 5th, 2026, and if you live in the Denver metro area, we’re going to have a great time, and you can get tickets here.

Finally, before we get started, we’re opening applications for our 2027 guided backpacking trips on Monday, December 7th. You can find more information here.

Footwear plays an outsized role in your backpacking trip. It’s one of the most important decisions that you’ll make as a backpacker when selecting your equipment, and just honestly, it’s really difficult to enjoy yourself on a backpacking trip if every step hurts or feels uncomfortable.

And it doesn’t help that the industry also uses a lot of technical jargon to describe their shoes. It can be pretty overwhelming if you’re not familiar with how shoes are constructed and the different features of them.

Before we talk about backpacking footwear, I think it would be worth looking at some of the other outdoor sports that we might be familiar with. So, for example, biking or skiing or running.

And if you know a serious skier or a serious biker or a serious runner, you know that they don’t have just one pair of shoes. They have multiple pairs of shoes. And when we talk about not just shoes, but multiple skis, multiple bikes, and when we talk about their collection or their quiver, they’ll say their quiver of skis. Each of those sets of skis, each of those bikes, has a specific application or purpose.

So, as an example, let me just kind of walk you through my little collection of running shoes.

Let me start with my trail running shoe. You’ll notice that the shoe has a narrower foot shape to give me good control laterally when I’m on uneven terrain. It also has an aggressive outsole for good traction on dirt and rocks.

This is what I would consider to be like my bike trail or gravel path trail shoe. The outsole is much less aggressive, and it’s a more comfortable upper. It’s a little bit wider, so I can run in the shoe for more time without feeling like my foot’s being constricted.

This is my road shoe. This outsole is almost nonexistent, has very little traction, and the big highlight feature about the shoe is all this thick foam so I can run on pavement without breaking my bones.

And then the last shoe I have with me, this is my racing shoe. This has some fancy, super high-tech foam that has a lot of energy rebound and makes me faster. And then the upper is also very minimalist, very minimally designed, very lightweight, so that it doesn’t slow me down over the course of those 26 miles.

So that’s just one example. These are my running shoes.

If you meet a biker or a skier, they’re gonna have the same thing. A skier’s gonna have their cross-country skis and their skate skis and their alpine skis and alpine touring skis, and then their, you know, you name it.

There’s, I think, what’s the saying? “n+1” is where you should stop buying skis.

And then bikers are gonna be the same way. They have their road bikes, their mountain bikes, their gravel bikes, their townies. Okay, same thing.

Now, interestingly, when we talk about backpacking shoes, there is really no consideration in the industry of what the optimal application is of a backpacking shoe.

If you look at backpackers who are on the cutting edge of backpacking, what you’ll see is that they don’t wear boots. The footwear that they choose isn’t actually designed for backpacking. The footwear that backpackers who are on the cutting edge of backpacking wear, they’re trail runners. They’re not even designed for backpacking. And it just happens to be probably the closest thing that is suitable for our particular activity.

What I want to emphasize to you when you’re looking at shoes, first, is that you want to be looking at what the optimal application of a particular shoe is.

So I’ll walk you through the features of a shoe and talk to you about the different conditions in which those sorts of features excel.

And then the other consideration when you’re buying a shoe is that you need to make sure that it fits you.

So at the end, we’ll talk about fit and how to make sure that it’s not just like the best backpacking shoe of 2026, but it’s the best backpacking shoe for the trip that you’re going on and that fits your foot.

Before we talk about how to pick a shoe for the right conditions, let’s actually talk about the parts of the shoe.

Let’s start with the outsole. This is going to be on the bottom of the shoe. It’s where literally the rubber meets the road.

It is going to be typically made out of some kind of rubber compound. There is a trade-off between sticky rubber and durable rubber. So just be aware of that trade-off when you are looking at shoes.

The other thing to be looking at with your footwear is going to be the lugs, including the lug pattern, the lug depth, and also the lug volume.

Generally speaking, for softer surfaces like mud or sand or snow, you want lugs that are taller and that have shorter edges to them.

And then the opposite is true, too. If you’re going to be walking on flat benched trails, your lug pattern doesn’t need to be as aggressive.

The next part of the shoe I want to talk about is the midsole. This is going to be made out of foam.

And when you’re thinking about the midsole, you want to be paying attention to the height of the midsole. And you sometimes might find the specs called stack height. So you might see like 30 millimeters on the heel and, say, 22 millimeters at the front.

The taller the shoe, the more wobbly it wants to be. However, the taller the shoe, the more cushioning you’re going to have. So that itself is a little bit of a trade-off.

Another thing you want to be thinking about with the midsole is just the firmness of this midsole.

This midsole here, this is a HOKA shoe. This is very plush, forgiving, soft, versus the midsole foam that’s used in this La Sportiva.

This is pretty firm. And that results in this shoe being more comfortable to wear and absorbing more of the ground, for example, like when you step on a rock.

However, this shoe gives you better ground feel. Your feet definitely feel more connected to the ground, more sensitive when you’re wearing this.

The third part of the shoe to be looking at is going to be the upper.

The upper, more than probably any other part of the shoe, is responsible for how this shoe fits you, so particularly its shape and its patterning.

It’s going to be made out of fabric and perhaps some faux leather.

The things to be looking at with the upper of a shoe are whether it’s breathable or whether it’s described as waterproof.

For some environments, you also want to be considering whether it’s sand-resistant.

And then the last thing is you want to be thinking about what the expected durability is of the upper.

In my experience, the upper of a shoe is typically the thing that blows out first. It develops holes. Sometimes if you snag it bad enough, a whole section of it can rip. Sometimes fabrics just kind of slowly fray apart.

So this is the first part of the shoe that blows out. You really want to give some consideration to this part of the shoe if you need to be using it for a very long distance.

So now that we’ve talked about the parts of the shoe, let’s actually talk about how different design decisions with each of these shoes make them optimized for particular environments.

The shoe that you choose for, say, the Appalachian Trail may not be the best shoe for, say, hiking in southern Utah. And the shoe for hiking in Utah might not be the best shoe for, say, hiking in the High Sierra or for Alaska.

So let’s talk about specific conditions that affect footwear choice.

Are you going to be hiking on compacted dirt or, like, coarsely ground mineral sand? Are you going to be hiking on slippery roots, rocks, and mud? Are you going to be walking on sharp talus and scree? Are you going to be walking through loose blow sand?

If you were planning a trail hike that was mostly on compacted dirt or ground-up mineral sand, such as what you might find on the East Coast or in the northern woodlands or, say, in the High Sierra, a shoe like this without a particularly aggressive outsole would be just fine.

However, if you are planning a trip where there’s going to be slippery rocks, roots, mud, slippery tundra, having an outsole with a more aggressive lug pattern would be better, something like this.

Or if you’re going to be, say, hiking on a bunch of slickrock or doing scrambling, you might want to be using some kind of shoe with an outsole like this where there’s flatter sections of rubber that give you more of an approach-style shoe with a lot of surface area that’s going to give you extra traction on those surfaces.

The next condition that you should be thinking about is going to be the typical surface angle of whatever you’re walking on.

So if you are walking on, say, a really high-quality benched trail, I’m thinking of something like the John Muir Trail, which is, even though it’s going up a lot, up and down a lot, it’s often switchbacking, and they have benched the trail into the mountains, so the trail itself is actually pretty flat.

In that case, a shoe, something like this, that kind of has a wide last on it, is going to be great because this is going to translate to more comfort.

It’s going to be a looser-fitting shoe. It’s going to give your foot places to move around, and you’re not going to be as prone to getting pinched or any constrictions in your shoe.

Of course, the classic example, and I don’t have a pair with me because I don’t wear them, would be the Altra Lone Peaks.

And for me, personally, it feels like I’m wearing clown shoes, but I totally get why they’re so popular among long-distance hikers who are on those really high-quality trails.

If you go to the other side of the spectrum, imagine you’re hiking off-trail in steep mountain country. It behooves you to have a shoe with a much narrower last.

This is the La Sportiva Bushido. There are a lot of people out there who cannot wear these because their feet just aren’t narrow enough, or they’re not willing to deal with the discomfort of having their foot kind of wedged into a shoe.

This is a much more appropriate shoe for a high route or for early-season conditions when you want that lateral stability of your shoe.

To take it to an even more extreme example, this is the La Sportiva TX Guide, and this is even a narrower last than the Bushido, which limits its application for, or suitability for, longer days on the trail, or just longer days in general, which makes it really excel in terrain that tends to be highly technical.

So we’ve talked about the walking surface. We’ve talked about the surface angle.

The last consideration that affects the suitability of footwear would just be a number of different environmental factors.

If you’re going to be in an area with a lot of spring runoff, so I’m thinking of, say, anywhere, like any mountain environment in the springtime, there tends to be high runoff of creeks. In that case, you’re going to be looking for a breathable shoe, not a waterproof shoe. (We’ll talk about waterproof shoes a little bit later and why my preference is for breathable shoes even in wet environments.)

Another environmental factor would be spring snow.

So if you’re going to be up in the mountains, say, in the West, if you’re going to be there in May or early June, you’re going to want a shoe with an aggressive outsole that’ll give you good bite and traction on that snow.

Another environmental consideration would be loose sand.

So I’m specifically thinking right now about the Four Corners of the United States or the western U.S. and places like southern Utah, which have that really fine, powdery blow sand.

There are shoes that, for example, like this one, are effectively sandproof even though they are still breathable.

And then there are other shoes, like this one, that are basically, they just, you wouldn’t believe the amount of sand that passes through the upper of a shoe like this.

So if you’re hiking in an area like that, you want to be aware of that consideration.

And then the last environmental factor that I would throw out there would be scratchy brush or vegetation.

This is gonna be more of an issue if you’re hiking off-trail, but anytime you’re walking through brush, so that might be willow or manzanita or chaparral, that is really hard on the uppers of the shoes.

So that’s a real argument then for going with a shoe that has a really durable, robust upper, not something that you would necessarily see on a road running shoe that has been given a few extra features to make it suitable for trail running.

So let’s rabbit hole for a few minutes about this discussion of waterproof or breathable shoes.

So-called waterproof shoes, they’re, it’s generous.

Let’s just assume that the shoe itself is waterproof. The problem is that there’s this big hole in the top of the shoe.

I would also argue that waterproof shoes don’t end up being waterproof long-term. Just the torsion that you put on a shoe from normal wear and tear breaks down that waterproof-breathable fabric.

The other thing that happens with waterproof shoes is that your feet get wet from the inside because of your perspiration.

So my recommendation to everyone has been my recommendation for, boy, going on like 20 years now.

Instead of trying to keep your feet dry in the backcountry, just embrace the fact that they’re going to get wet and learn how to manage wet feet.

Let me give you four ways that you can manage wet feet in the backcountry.

First, you can wear a breathable shoe. A breathable shoe will expel water faster, and it will also dry out faster because more air will be passing through the shoe, not just through the top, but also moving through the fabric in the upper.

Second, when you are taking a break during the day, so at rest stops, remove your shoes and socks. Just five minutes, 10 minutes, 15 minutes of fresh air to your feet can be really nurturing for your feet that otherwise are just squeezed into a wet shoe all day long.

Third, at night you want to have a dedicated pair of sleeping socks, and you want to make sure that your feet get warm and dry at night and that they stay that way.

Fourth, you can bring out a balm. So this is GurneyGoo. This is just one example of many.

And use this balm to recondition your feet at night. The way that you do it is you get to camp, you do all your dinner stuff, you go into your tent, you take off shoes and socks, you let your feet dry out for a while and get them nice and dry, and at that point you put the balm on.

And that’ll basically recondition your skin, and then in the morning the bottoms of your feet will feel a lot more restored than if you had just let them air out and get really dry and crispy over the course of the night.

Quick plug that we have done a couple of videos in the past about managing wet feet in the backcountry.

At the top, I made the argument that your footwear needs to do two things for you.

The first is that it needs to be appropriate for the conditions and optimized for your application. And then the second thing that it needs to do for you is that it needs to fit.

Footwear fit is highly personalized. It’s really unlike any other type of backpacking clothing or anything else that you wear.

So as an example, if I took this shirt, which isn’t a backpacking shirt, but it’s going to convey the idea.

If I took the shirt and I gave it to another male who’s about six feet tall and 160 pounds, it probably would fit them just fine and they could wear the shirt.

However, if I were to give them this shoe, this size 11-and-a-half shoe, they might put it on and it might fit them horribly.

And that’s because fit is very personalized.

And there are a couple of things going on here.

Part of it is the shape of your particular foot, like whether you have, say, a narrow heel and a big toe splay, or maybe something that’s a little bit more ruler-shaped.

Also, what is the volume of your foot? Are your arches high? Are they low?

In addition to the shape of your foot, your gait plays into this as well.

What exactly is that process of lifting your foot in the air and putting it back on the ground? Are you pronating? Are you landing flat? There’s just lots of options in this.

Another consideration with fit is going to be your Achilles, the flexibility and strength of your Achilles tendon.

And that’s going to affect how much tolerance you have for this angle, whether you can do a right angle, or whether, like with a zero drop, you’re actually a little bit more than a right angle, or whether you need something with a little bit of a lift so that it’s a greater-than-90-degree angle.

That just is impacted by, again, the flexibility and strength of your Achilles.

And then finally, the last thing with fit is just personal preference.

Like my tolerance for squeezing my foot into something like that La Sportiva TX Guide might be higher than the next person who just isn’t willing to deal with that constant squeezing tightness for the performance gains that those shoes offer in particular environments.

So how should footwear fit you? What are you looking for?

The first is that when you put it on, out of the box, it should immediately be comfortable. It should feel like someone had a mold of your foot, and they created a shoe just for you. It shouldn’t pinch. It shouldn’t be applying pressure in places where you don’t want it. And you should be able to be very comfortable in the shoe, again, out of the box without aftermarket insoles.

I know a lot of people like their aftermarket insoles. I’ve just always felt that the designers design the shoe for a foot, and the aftermarket insole changes the intended user, and you probably shouldn’t do that. You should probably just go find another shoe.

And then the other criteria for determining whether a shoe fits you really well is you take a walk around the shoe store or around your house and try, if available, to go up and down some things. That way, you can replicate more of a true hiking environment.

And you want to feel like you can walk naturally in that shoe, that your heel-to-toe transition feels really natural, that it’s locking your heel down the way you want it to. It should feel like you’re walking in a slipper in an optimal situation.

Now, the one little caveat that I would add to fit is not every shoe is going to feel super comfortable right out of the box. And that’s just going to be a trade-off of the shoe.

So, for example, when I put on these La Sportiva shoes, this feels comfortable right out of the box. It feels like a slipper. It feels like it’s made for me.

I put on these TX Guides, I’m like, okay, they’re a little tight. I wouldn’t mind a little bit more room. But I just know that in certain environments these shoes are phenomenal.

And I’m willing to deal with maybe some slight discomfort, nothing that’s going to be trip-ending, just something where it’s more like at the end of the day, I’m looking forward to taking them off.

The other thing about fit and comfort: if you’re trying on two different sizes, and one size almost fits you perfectly, you just feel like it’s a little bit tight, and then you go up to the next half size, and that definitely feels too big, I would actually lean more towards the smaller shoe.

What you’ll find is that most shoes have enough foam in them, and maybe even some, not just foam in the midsole, but also some open-cell foam in the heel cup.

And those materials pack out a little bit with use, and that will give you that extra breathing room that you’re looking for.

Typically for backpacking, your backpacking shoes are going to be about a half size bigger than casual shoes.

So, for example, I’m size 11 in these shoes. I’m size 11-and-a-half in these shoes. That’s pretty typical.

You need a little bit more room in your backpacking shoes to account for foot swelling.

The other thing is you don’t want your feet jamming into the front of your shoe, assuming that you like to keep your toenails.

When you’re sizing your shoe, one way that I typically will do it, I’m going to demonstrate here, is I’ll put on a shoe, and I’ll jam my feet into the toe box. And if I can get my index finger down the back of the shoe, that typically is about right.

It shouldn’t be a really easy depression into the back there, but you want some friction on it.

But you shouldn’t be able to wiggle around and not be in contact on both sides of your index finger.

If you find that the shoe mostly fits, but you need just a little bit of extra heel lock, you can mess around with your lacing systems.

So, for example, let me bring this close.

This is a heel lock that I added to the very back of that shoe.

Another thing that I sometimes do, if you’ve been watching these videos, sometimes what I’ll do is I’ll basically just remove the lacing from this part of the shoe, and that relieves the pressure over that bunion.

So you can mess around with your lacing system if you need to kind of optimize the fit of your particular shoes.

Like you found the shoe, it’s almost perfect. You’re just trying to really get that last five or 10% of fit in.

We’ve talked about fit. We’ve talked about sizing. Let’s next talk about the toe box.

This topic gets a ton of attention in the backpacking community. And understandably, because backpackers, they’re spending like 10, 12 hours a day in these shoes, putting lots of miles on them, and some discomfort caused by the toe box can have big repercussions.

So just realize right now that there is a trade-off.

If you go with a very wide toe box or something like an Altra, which is going to, you know, come out to here, yes, that gives you a lot of room in that toe box.

It allows your toes to splay, and how wonderful that feels.

But it also leads to a really sloppy fit. You are going to be moving around in that shoe if you’re putting that shoe on level walking surfaces.

So the Altra Lone Peak, classic thru-hiking shoe, and also highly appropriate to trails like, say, the Pacific Crest Trail.

Probably less appropriate for something like the Appalachian Trail.

And when we see our clients, some of our clients come out with the Lone Peaks and try to take them off-trail in places like Alaska or southern Utah and walk on steep slickrock or steep alpine tundra or off-trail in the Sierra on high routes, they just don’t work that well.

Some clients can do them, can wear them. They’re capable in them. But for a lot of clients, it just leads to unsteadiness that isn’t helpful. So in that case, you want to be looking at a shoe with a narrower toe box.

And again, you’re dealing with that trade-off of a narrower toe box.

It’s going to be a little bit less comfortable, but you need to be considering, or your thinking needs to be, am I willing to endure the tightness and constriction of that narrow toe box for increased performance?

Or do I not need that performance, in which case I can go with that really wide toe box?

So the last topic when it comes to fit is going to be the heel-to-toe drop.

If you have a zero-drop shoe, so this shoe, this is just an Adidas street shoe. This is, I believe, a zero-drop shoe.

Which means that the stack height of the midsole and the outsole in the heel is the same as it is in the forefoot.

That’s a very natural style of footwear. Like if you were barefoot, that’s what it would be. You’d have zero drop.

To the contrary, this shoe here, this is a road marathon shoe. And I believe this has a heel drop of 12 millimeters. I think it’s 28 or 30 in the back and maybe 20 in the front.

And as a backpacker, here’s the way to think about this.

If you have a zero-drop shoe, your lower leg is at a right angle, or at least it wants to be.

But when you put a backpack on, you actually need to be leaning forward to counteract or counterbalance the weight of that backpack.

So if you are wearing zero-drop shoes when backpacking, just realize you’re going to probably forward lean and your Achilles is going to be at something less than a 90-degree angle. That could put a lot of stress and strain on your Achilles, especially if you’re not used to it. That would be the big thing.

You can build up to it, but if you’re accustomed to shoes like this, or running shoes or trail shoes or any kind of shoes that have big heel-to-toe drops, and then you try to get in a zero-drop shoe, there is a buildup period.

It is going to hurt if you just suddenly start trying to put on lots of miles.

I think for backpacking, probably the most natural heel-to-toe drop is going to be something in a moderate range. So maybe six millimeters, maybe eight millimeters, because that kind of naturally lifts the shoe a little bit. So, for example, the shoe would be lifted because the heel would have some more rubber under it.

And at that point, you’re able to be at still a little bit of a forward lean, but that forward lean creates only a 90-degree angle, or maybe even a little bit less than a 90-degree angle. So it’s gonna put less strain on your Achilles.

So that’s a personal preference thing. But I do think probably naturally some mild drop is probably best for most people.

When you’re buying shoes, the heel-to-toe drop would not be on my top three list of considerations.

I think that you’re mostly looking for just that out-of-the-box comfort, how well the sizing works for you, and the shape of that toe box.

That heel drop is probably pretty far down the list.

That was a lot of shoes and a lot of foot talk.

But hopefully at this point, you have a better sense of what you’re looking for when you’re selecting backpacking shoes for your next trip.

#8336. Everest Winds Force Climbers to Retreat

Despite weather forecasts of high winds on Everest, the Russian team gamely left for the West Shoulder today. But in the end, they couldn’t fight the gale-force winds, which eventually forced them back to their tent at the base of the mountain.

“It’s calm right now, but the forecast is worrying,” leader Andrei Vasiliev wrote yesterday from a snow cave, where Sergey Kondrashkin, Vitaly Shiilov, and Vasiliev had sheltered for the night at 6,400m. Late today, they confirmed that the weather forecast of powerful winds was unfortunately right.

The three climbers had fixed three pitches above them, according to Mountain Club, and were hoping for firmer terrain as they approached the West Shoulder of Everest, en route to the West Ridge.

However, as we previously reported after consulting meteorologist Vitor Baia of Portugal, conditions were good Tuesday and Wednesday, but winds rose dramatically today.

On a previous foray up Everest, one of the climbers heads for the West Shoulder. Photo: Andrei Vasiliev

“The winds are very strong,” Vasiliev reported. “It is impossible to work higher on the mountain.”

Vasiliev said that his team is retreating to a tent at 5,600m. This is below the Lho La Pass, possibly on the Khumbu Glacier beside the vertical section leading to that col.

The question is whether the team will wait there for better conditions, retreat to their base in Lobuche, or call off the expedition.

Some charts still show a chance of variable winds tomorrow, before the jet stream hits the mountain with full force (see below).

Wind levels at around summit altitude on Everest this week, by meteoexploration.com

Baia says the winds will remain high every day until next Tuesday. From Wednesday on, the winds should be weaker.

The Russians set off on their third trip up Everest last Monday, hoping to summit via the West Ridge without oxygen or external support, and with no one else on the mountain.

On Tuesday evening, they checked in with Mountain Club from their tent at 6,000m, where they spent the night. The hard trailbreaking through fresh snow had slowed their pace, but they expected better conditions on the following day, as they started up steeper terrain toward Everest’s shoulder. They knew the snow up there was harder and compacted. They could even see their trails from the previous trip up, so no new soft snow had accumulated.

The Russian team on Everest’s West Shoulder. Photo: Andrei Vasiliev

Yesterday, Vasiliev noted that they had climbed four pitches on old fixed ropes, plus another eight pitches of their own. The team is fixing ropes at the hardest sections, but not entirely along the route. As expected, snow conditions had improved with the more vertical terrain, but as Vasiliev pointed out, “it’s not exactly easy walking yet.”

The Russian leader insists the route to the summit is doable. However, wind delays and a lack of time may ultimately end their attempt.

#8333. This Traditional Archer Tagged a Big 8-Pointer With a Longbow and Stone Point Arrowhead He Made Himself

Published Nov 9, 2026 2:20 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

It was the evening of October 25 when Joe McConnell, a traditional bowhunter from central Ohio, climbed into a ladder stand with a backdrop of heavy cover.

McConnell was hunting with a longbow in a narrow strip of woods that he owns. The spot was overlooking a travel lane deer were using to enter a large field of standing corn.

“I was really after a nice 10-point buck I had on trail cameras,” McConnell tells Outdoor Life. “The wind was perfect that evening, blowing right in my face. I was looking forward to filling my first landowner tag with a deer on my own property.”

“I’d made a mock scrape in front of my stand, with a licking branch above it about 12 yards from my ladder,” says the 38-year-old archer. “I heard him in the corn about six rows in, and he came to check the scrape.

“I saw his rack above the standing corn field coming to me, and when he stopped broadside quartering away at 12 yards, I took my shot.”

The buck was not the 10-point he was after. Instead, it was a dandy 8-pointer that McConnell couldn’t pass up.

McConnell drew his 64-inch, 55-pound longbow he’d made from an Osage orange tree limb. Then, shooting instinctively, he released a 26-inch arrow he’d made of cane and fletched with white turkey feathers.

His arrow hit the buck perfectly behind the shoulder, the super-sharp stone broadhead he handmade drove deep into the deer’s chest.

“I saw him race and tear through the corn field,” says McConnell, who owns a hardwood flooring and restoration business. “I heard him crashing away through corn stalks, and then nothing.”

The bowman found a good blood trail where the buck stood, and the trail was easy to follow through the corn.

“He only went about 75 yards before falling dead,” he says. “My stone broadhead did an incredible job, driving through its liver, a lung, and smashing into a rib on the opposite side of the buck. The arrow point hit bone and stopped or it would have completely gone through the buck’s hide on the opposite side.

“Everything really came together for me with that deer. I’ve taken lots of animals with the bows and arrows I’ve made. But it’s the first deer I’ve taken with a stone broadhead that I knapped myself – and it was devastating.”

McConnell has taken plenty of deer and wild hogs with traditional archery gear he’s made. He also shot a buffalo with his traditional equipment, and a stone point head he’d made specifically for the hunt.

He called some friends who helped get his big 8-point buck out of the field. The field-dressed buck weighed 180 pounds at the processor. It had an estimated live weight of well over 200 pounds.

He will eventually have the deer scored via Buckmasters, and his taxidermist estimated the rack to be about 140 inches.

Read Next: Killing a Grizzly the Old-Fashioned Way: With a Longbow and a Stone-Point Arrowhead

McConnell models his canted bow shooting style after legendary archer Howard Hill. He leans forward when drawing his bow, anchors his arrow at the corner of his mouth, and uses his bare hand holding his bow as an arrow rest.

“I got started in archery when I was only 14,” McConnell says. “It’s been a lot of fun learning about making bows, arrows, and now stone broadheads. Traditional equipment is remarkably efficient bowhunting gear.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

#8331. Piolet d'Or Team Shares Insider Tips for Climbing in Peru's Huayhuash Range

Marc Toralles, Ruben Sanmartin, and Bru Busom of Spain were awarded a Piolet d’Or for a highly committing new route on Yerupaja Grande (6,634m), the highest peak of Peru’s Huayhuash range. ExplorersWeb asked the young climbers about the ascent and the unique character of the Andes. They offered some tips for climbers looking to add Peru to their list of high-altitude destinations.

In the last few years, the Andes have reportedly been affected by climate change. “The main effect is that the huge snow structures on the summits are exposed to warmer temperatures and become more fragile,” Sanmartin said. “Basically, they fall down more easily.”

“That means that climbers need to pick their route carefully [when judging] what lingers above them,” Buson explained.

Bru Buson, left, and Marc Toralles. Photo: Ruben Sanmartin

This was especially noticeable last year on Siula Grande. “There was a snowfield midway up the face as we trekked by, and on our way back, it was not there anymore,” said Marc Toralles. “Somehow, the melting process in the Andes, with its 5,000m and even 6,000m [altitudes], is similar to what we are seeing every year in the Alps, where we all work as mountain guides.”

For Toralles, the best thing in the Andes is how accessible the mountains are. “It’s so easy to go climbing there,” he said.

There are no lifts or paved roads, but you can go, climb a peak, and return in three or four days. Something like that in the Himalaya would take weeks. All you need is to get to Huaraz by plane, bus, or car from Lima, where you may stay some days to acclimatize (there are many options), and then go to the mountains.

If your planned peak is not in the right conditions, you will surely find some other one to climb. Also, the options are infinite for ice climbing, alpine climbing, rock, trekking, anything.

The team’s base camp. Photo: Spanish Yerupaja Grande team

Red tape is minimal. There are no permits for peaks. Instead, climbers and trekkers pay a small fee to cross the comunidades (the areas) where they want to climb, and climbers can hire arrieros (riders with mules) to help carry equipment and supplies to base camps or higher. On this occasion, the climbers kept their logistics basic, with little beyond a slightly larger base camp tent.

With some of the more accessible places, it is advisable to have someone watching over your base camp while you are away climbing.

Once you leave the beaten track, you’ll be alone. “There are no crowds in Huayhuash,” Buson said. Why aren’t they more popular? The climbers explained that Huayhuash peaks are typically more difficult than popular options in the Cordillera Blanca, also in Peru. But they note this lack of popularity is changing fast. “We are seeing much more people in recent years, both climbing and trekking,” they said.

Thanks to the many possibilities, the three Spanish climbers didn’t need to do much planning. “We know Huayhuash pretty well, we are familiar with the terrain and the climbing style, and we are aware there are always options,” said Toralles, who has climbed there four times.

That said, the line the Spaniards climbed on Yerupaja last year was not improvised. They had that route in their mind for some time. “We heard of someone who had tried but called the attempt off, so the route was there to be climbed,” Toralles said. “In addition, it didn’t require a long time, and the timing worked for me, since I was traveling to Peru anyway to do some guiding.”

Buson and Sanmartin joined immediately. To acclimatize, they had worked double shifts the previous weeks around Chamonix, where both are professional guides. “Working on the Mont Blanc massif intensively was great training and acclimatization,” they said. “We were ready by the time we set foot on the mountain.”

The best time to climb in Huayhuash is July, August, and the first half of September.

Once on the mountain, they carefully checked the conditions of the face and the line they would follow. “In the Andes, you have to assume that the summit ridge snow formations, the famous ‘mushrooms,’ the big cornices, and the seracs on the sides of the face are mostly in a delicate state. You need to assume that you will progress on deep, unstable snow, and the entire structure may have deteriorated and be prone to crumble,” Buson noted.

To deal with them, the climbers used some custom-made tools: They attached alitas (little wings) to their ice axes for a better grip on foamy, loose snow. They also carried small snowshoes for some sections.

Ice axe with ‘alitas’ (wings). Photo: Nick Sheldon/Mountain Project

Conditions in the Andean mountains are always variable. “You’ll find different terrain depending on the altitude and the orientation,” Sanmartin added. “This is why technical routes on these peaks require a high level of commitment, which, as happened on our route, may be even higher than the intrinsic difficulty of the pitches,” Toralles explained.

With this in mind, the Spanish team named the route they opened Essence of Commitment.

Marc Toralles ready for the ridge, with large snow pickets and an ice axe with ‘wings’ attached. Photo: Buson/Sanmartin

In this sort of terrain, it is paramount to stay focused and make the right call between subjective and objective factors. “There are countless reasons to turn around on climbs like this, but one must keep going, except for obvious reasons, such as an injury, a completely impassable section, or a really violent storm,” Sanmartin said.

“That is why it is important to consider all options and potential issues before starting the climb. You need to be sure you like the line and want to climb it.”

Asked whether they reached a point of no return during their climb, Buson admitted they did. “On reaching the ridge, we became aware that trying to downclimb the same route would be quite complex. However, we also saw the route upward was doable.”

The Spaniards did not count on a professional meteorologist. “We had our friends texting us the online, free forecasts over InReach,” they said. “One of the positive aspects of Huayhuash is that good weather periods tend to last for quite a while. Weather windows of five to six days are common.” During their climb, for example, they enjoyed acceptable weather, with some rain and snow but no high winds.

They warn that conditions in the Andes are changing quickly. “You need to observe the mountains and adapt,” Toralles said. “Some people aim for a route based on the information in a guidebook printed 50 years ago, and find a totally different scenario.” He pointed out that Siula Grande looked different in 2025 than it did when he climbed it just three years earlier.

Sanmartin noted that conditions have also changed on normal routes. They are now drier, more complex, and often more hazardous. “In some cases, climbers are abandoning the normal routes up the glaciers and opt for ridges which may be more difficult but in better conditions,” he said.

The climbers think the Andes will not get as crowded as the Himalaya, simply because the logistics are not as advanced — no refuges, no helicopters — and because they don’t have the sizzle of being the tallest peaks on Earth. “But they have incredible potential,” they said.

#8329. Tips for Hunting Mature Bucks Over Bait (Plus the Ethics of If You Should)

Published Nov 9, 2026 4:16 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

During the 2023 deer season, I shot my biggest buck ever: a Kentucky main-frame 12-pointer with a couple extra points that scored 168 6/8 inches. And I shot that big sum-buck over a yellow pile of ear and persimmon-flavored shelled corn. I even dropped a protein block in the middle and seasoned it with scent for good measure.

And as I write this, I can hear blood boiling and I can see the angry comments coming. Hunting whitetails over bait is still a controversial topic across much of deer country. It is, of course, illegal in many states in the Midwest, Northeast, and West. And even in places where baiting is legal, some argue that hunters should avoid using it.

The National Deer Association published an article explaining how baiting deer can spread disease: “By design, baiting and supplemental feeding concentrate deer and other wildlife species for the purposes of hunting, trapping, viewing, or simply increasing nutrition available to the animals. Some argue baiting and feeding have positive attributes, but no one can disagree they have numerous negative implications to a deer herd, including altering animal behavior, degrading the habitat near bait and feed sites, increasing the susceptibility to predation, and greatly enhancing the opportunity for disease transmission.”

The NDA’s formal position on baiting deer is this: “The NDA opposes the expansion of baiting where not currently legal. The NDA will not work to repeal baiting where currently legal, except where CWD (or other known diseases) is present. The NDA supports the use of baiting by wildlife professionals conducting scientific research. The NDA supports continued research on the effects on baiting in deer management programs.”

Baiting is legal and very popular in many Southern states. All hell broke loose when Kansas regulators began discussing the potential of banning deer baiting. Hunting deer in Texas is synonymous with hunting over a feeder. But still, many folks don’t understand how big bucks relate to bait piles.

Two comments I always hear when the bait debate pops up go something like: “Oh, you bait, bro? Must be easy killing those big bucks.” Or, “Bait won’t kill big, mature bucks, Jack.”

I scoff at both of those statements. Neither are true, and it’s far easier to screw up on a mature buck with a poor baiting strategy than it is to fill a tag with one. Like other tactics, baiting is just another tool in the arsenal. It isn’t a cheat code.

I know because I’ve been baiting Kentucky whitetails most of my hunting career, and it took a long time before I started experiencing success on mature deer. Here is how to bait deer and, more specifically, how to bait a monster buck—plus some considerations if you’re on the fence about legal baiting.

The early season is my favorite time to bait a monster buck. Hunting pressure is low. There’s plenty of cover for concealment. Deer are on predictable bed-to-feed lines of movement. There’s no better time to catch a buck off-guard over a bait pile.

Target warm-season bedding areas, such as north-facing slopes or low-lying areas near water that offer a high stem count. Then, determine what destination food source deer are targeting, such as green soybeans, fresh-cut corn, or dropping white oak acorns. Place the bait between the bedroom and dinner table.

Don’t overlook the pre-rut. This is a great time to bait a monster buck. Bucks are moving more but aren’t running wild just yet. So, capitalize on that buck’s rising testosterone levels. Often that leads to increased daylight travel.

As with the early season, it’s still all about bed-to-feed patterns. But now, deer are likely settling into their fall bedding areas and food sources. So, find these locations (which vary greatly by region and habitat type) and plant bait stations along lines of travel.

Once the rut hits, you might get a buck to hit a bait pile. But most of the time, a rutting buck is more interested in scent-checking does that have been through the buffet line. Because of this, bait stations are still effective during the rut, but you must hunt them differently.

Usually, I prefer to place bait piles on the downwind sides of doe bedding areas. Or locate the bait between two doe bedding areas. Bucks like to travel these locations anyway, so it’s a no-brainer game plan.

Once the rut is over, and the late season arrives, it’s all about the food again. But still, focus on late-season bedding areas. Think solar bedding (south-facing slopes) and thermal cover (dense stands of coniferous trees). Also, consider what deer are eating now, including remaining waste grain, green plots, remaining acorns, but most importantly, woody browse.

Once again, the best bet is placing bait piles between bedding areas and destination food sources. Use these as staging areas to intercept bucks during daylight. At this point, they aren’t likely to reach destination food sources before dark. But if your bait site is located close enough to bedding, they might hit that corn pile during legal shooting hours.

Read Next: 35 Deer Hunting Tips to Read Before This Season

No matter the time of year, there are certain tactics that apply to virtually every baiting scenario. Of course, you have to locate a big buck before bait will help you. No volume of bait will bring in a monster buck if he doesn’t already live there. But once you’ve got a big buck located, follow these tips.

Some people say deer hunting over bait isn’t ethical. But here’s the thing: Some hunting ethics are situational. I’m not talking about taking poor shots or breaking hunting laws. Those ethics are clearly black and white.

For me, baiting deer is more of a gray area. First off, if it’s legal, it’s legal. When analyzing the differences of state-to-state guidelines on baiting, you’ll see regional discrepancies just like you will with crossbow regulations, the use of deer urine lures, and season structures. So if your area allows baiting, then it’s likely that agency wildlife managers for that area have decided baiting is not detrimental to the deer and deer hunting.

Second, it’s important to realize that it’s not like you can just throw out bait at any random spot in the woods and start shooting trophy bucks. It’s far from a canned hunt. If you live in an area that permits baiting, understand that bait has several different applications. Where legal, it can be used to help you attract deer, hold deer, feed supplemental nutrition, take inventory of deer, and more.

As for effectiveness, people commonly say bait stations are either fool-proof or that they turn bucks nocturnal. In my experience, neither are true.

Whitetails are smart, especially around bait piles. They learn to approach bait piles downwind and they pick up on hunters’ scent in the area. Yes, they will often hit bait at night. But no buck is 100 percent nocturnal—that’s a myth.

If you suddenly start seeing a target buck avoiding bait during daylight, it’s likely because you screwed up and disrupted that deer. Or, he started bedding elsewhere. Or, he started targeting a different destination food source. Or, maybe he’s just being a wild, unpredictable deer.

The point is, hunting over bait is still deer hunting. Anything can happen, and most of the time the big buck wins. But if you understand how to bait deer and you execute a well-crafted game plan, then baiting just might lead to the biggest whitetail of your life.

#8327. Traveling 290 Miles on Foot in 4 Days? John Kelly Sets New Benton Mackaye Trail FKT

Ultrarunner John Kelly, 42, set an unsupported and overall Fastest Known Time (FKT) on the Benton Mackaye Trail last month, completing the 288-mile route through the southern Appalachians in 4 days, 5 hours, and 48 minutes. “Unsupported” means Kelly carried all his own food and gear for the entire journey and received no outside help. His pack weighed 27 pounds on day one, with roughly 50% of the weight coming from food.

When he completed the trek on October 22nd, Kelly became the fastest person ever to complete the Benton Mackaye Trail (BMT), even besting the supported speed record of 4 days, 21 hours, and 25 minutes set by Tara Dower in 2022. Supported records are normally faster than unsupported ones because athletes can carry less weight and benefit from support crews.

He also beat the previous unsupported speed record set by Bob Stewart in May of this year (5 days, 5 hours, and 21 minutes) by more than 24 hours.

The BMT starts at Springer Mountain, Georgia and ends at Big Creek in Great Smoky Mountains National Park. According to Kelly’s own tracking data, he climbed nearly 63,000 feet, burned 38,000 calories, and slept for just 6 and a half hours total in his four-plus days on trail.

Kelly’s trip report indicated that he only committed to the BMT attempt one month ahead of time and told no one except his wife, his coach, and Bob Stewart, who held the unsupported FKT at that time. (“He was so genuinely kind, helpful, and thrilled to see someone getting out on this trail,” Kelly wrote of Stewart’s reaction when Kelly told him he was coming for his record.)

He started the trek on October 18 and finished on October 22 but didn’t publicize his tracking data and trip report until eight days later. “I considered keeping this quiet. You know, just go for a hike like most people do. But I knew this would also be one of the best ways I could continue my AT fundraiser for Western Youth Network, supporting kids in western North Carolina,” he wrote. He has raised $15,000 for the nonprofit and is matching donations 100%.

Kelly has previously set FKTs on trails including the UK’s Pennine Way and Vermont’s Long Trail. He is also one of only a few dozen athletes ever to complete the infamous Barkley Marathons and one of only three to complete the course more than once (he finished in 2017, 2023, and 2024).

In his BMT trip report, Kelly described experiencing auditory hallucinations on the way down from the final summit, running out of food with 5 hours to go in the trek, visualizing a large meal at Cookout to keep himself going, and regretting not packing more Nerds gummy clusters.

Kelly credited his wife, the crews who maintain the trail, and fellow athletes like Bob Stewart with making his record possible. “Even unsupported,” he wrote, “it’s not really solo.”

#8326. These Popular Bass Fishing Myths Were Debunked Decades Ago

Published Nov 9, 2026 5:04 PM EST

The hottest fishing news, tips, and tactics

“Missed him,” I said and groaned as I reeled in a slack line. Setting the hook after I had felt a gentle tap had produced nothing but a mangled plastic worm.

“Rerig and toss it back in there,” Ralph Manns directed me. “Another one might hit it.”

Manns was right. The next time I set the hook I was rewarded with a pleasantly bowed rod. After a short fight, a five-pound largemouth bass thrashed in the landing net.

“Now, you get one,” I said and grinned as my bass splashed in the live well.

Manns complied with a cast that dropped his worm beside the same rotted stump. The line twitched, the rod arched and he quickly landed a twin to the first bass. Not a bad start for our first look at Texas’ Lake Livingston—especially after my traumatic and almost sleepless night.

It had begun innocently enough with my arrival at Livingston’s Penwaugh Marina the previous evening for a meeting of the Texas Outdoor Writers Association. Luck of the draw had placed me in the same room with fisheries biologist Ralph Manns, a pleasant mild-mannered gentleman I was meeting for the first time. I had no idea that our meeting would alter the course of my fishing life.

An insatiable appetite for more facts about bass behavior had prompted Manns to become a full-time student at Southwest Texas State University after retiring from active duty in the Air Force where he had spent 20 years as an intelligence officer and a political scientist. At the time of his retirement he was a lieutenant colonel.

En route to a masters of science degree in aquatic biology, which he earned in 1981, Manns conducted a study of black bass in Lake Travis in 1977 and 1978. The study team used electronic transmitter tags and receivers to track 14 bass while divers recorded the behavior of another 935 bass. Manns’ team also used angling techniques to obtain detailed reports on an additional 289 bass. Data on more than 20 different types of behavior and the environmental conditions that might influence the fish were fed into a computer to compile and analyze the results.

Manns’ exhaustive studies of scientific literature on bass and his extensive two-year study have led to rather innovative and sometimes controversial conclusions. During my first few hours with him, he crumbled the foundation of my bass fishing knowledge with solid scientific evidence that contradicted many generally accepted fishing theories.

Those first two bass we caught from Lake Livingston, for example, were taken from classic bass structure—a stump on the upper edge of a submerged creek bed. The bass probably had not migrated from the depths, however, to feed in the shallower water as a popular myth would have us believe.

The hypothesis that bass live in deep water, migrate to shallow water on a daily basis to feed and then return to deep water along established migration routes is generally credited to Buck Perry, the gentleman rightfully recognized as the father of structure fishing. His methods of catching bass have been so successful that other anglers and writers have perpetuated his migration theories.

Ralph Manns agreed that such structures are good places in which to fish but the analytically minded biologist could find no scientific literature to substantiate a deep-water migration theory. Manns wanted a better explanation for the appearance of bass at certain underwater structure.

“Sure enough,” Manns related, “our tracking studies verified data available in at least 15 other studies that showed the predominant movements of black bass are parallel to the shorelines and contour lines and not perpendicular to them. The movement, moreover, usually is at a nearly constant depth.”

A national magazine recently published a story entitled “When the Sun is High, the Bass Go Deep”. The author surmised that because more bass are caught in deep water during midday hours, bass obviously migrate from shallow to deep water as the sun brightens. And that’s how myths get started.

Manns’ study of Lake Travis bass showed no such tendency.

“Our research showed some bass at shallow depths, some at medium depths and some in deep water at all times,” he revealed. “And they tended to remain at that particular depth whether active or inactive. Bass do move vertically but not routinely. A deep fish might pop to the surface to run through a school of shad, but it will grab two or three and almost immediately dive to its original depth because that’s where it has established its equilibrium and can suspend without effort.”

A bass can hover effortlessly at a particular depth because its air bladder is balanced at that depth and it gets out of balance if it varies much from that point. According to Manns, it takes hours for the air bladder to adjust to a major depth change. The more out of balance a bass gets, the harder it has to swim to maintain its stability.

Manns noted that black bass usually move along contour lines for only short distances and tend to stop at any bisecting lines of structure that obstruct their movement, such as a fence, stump or rock. By casting to a fence, an angler increases his odds simply because moving bass are likely to pause there.

Most anglers will catch more bass, Manns maintained, if they fish on the better bisecting structures for several minutes during activity periods. Additional schools often stop at the same structure long enough for the anglers to catch more fish.

Some fishermen consider schools of black bass a rarity. Limit catches might be rare but, according to Manns’ underwater observations, most bass are in either a school or an aggregation.

“There are almost no loner bass,” Manns says. “The few single fish we observed underwater were either very sick, very large or totally inactive. The sick ones obviously couldn’t keep up and the larger bass were probably unable to find compatible partners in their size range.

“Rule one,” he continued, “if you catch a bass, assume it is not alone. As a minimum, it is in a group of two to five fish. Actively striking bass are usually feeding and moving bass. They tighten up their aggregations and move in small schools.”

During activity periods, of course, almost anyone can catch bass by tossing an edible-looking lure in the path of a feeding school. It’s the inactive periods that separate expert from novice bass fishermen.

“Fishermen should remember that bass are inactive most of the time, even if their stomachs are empty,” Manns claimed. “Unlike humans who have a predictable three square meals a day, a bass may be forced to exist for days without eating. That little bit of minnow it ate three days ago must last the bass a long time and it does not waste it!”

The fact that a fish is inactive doesn’t mean that it won’t eat, Manns admitted. If something passes closely and in front of a bass’ mouth, it will usually eat because bass are opportunists. But an inactive bass probably won’t move more than a few inches to hit a lure. An angler who is fishing a spot that holds inactive bass may have to cast 15 or more times before the lure passes through that tiny strike zone in front of an inactive bass.

A fast-moving crankbait offers good odds of getting a lure to an active bass. Because active fish may chase lures for 10 feet or more, each cast effectively covers a wide path on each side of the bait’s track. The same lure retrieved past inactive bass, however, may pass by many fish without eliciting a strike.

The success of an angler’s technique, then, depends on how active the bass are. Anglers who cast and crank at wide intervals over inactive bass will usually get few strikes. Fishermen who flip worms or jigs to the base of cover objects where inactive bass may hold, however, get close enough to take a few fish. If the same anglers continue to flip slow lures while bass are in an active state, though, they cannot hope to hook as many as those with fast lures. Thus, an angler must match his lure and his technique to the activity level of the bass.

One widely accepted myth claims that bass avoid sunlight because they have no eyelids. Manns’ underwater observations indicate otherwise.

“It was clear to us that bass have no requirement to live in shade,” Manns declared. “Moving bass were as likely to stop at a sunny spot as a shaded one. Although bass have no eyelids, their eyes have pigment mechanisms that compensate for excessive light. Water is an excellent light filter, too. Unless a bass is in very shallow, calm and clear water, the water serves it well as a pair of sunglasses.”

Bass that anglers catch from beneath piers, boat houses and docks are probably there for cover or food rather than for shade. In fact, even slightly colored water refracts sunlight sufficiently to destroy any distinct shade line within a few feet. Even in moderately clear lakes, bass down more than 10 feet experience little or no shading from objects at the surface.

This means that bass a fisherman finds beneath floating structures will probably hold tightly to the bottom of the structure—unless, of course, there are other attractive structural elements on the bottom. Lures retrieved just beneath floating structures, then, will probably be more effective than lures retrieved along the bottom.

Although bass do not need to avoid sunlight, Manns’ records indicate that more bass are caught from shaded areas than from sunny ones. He stressed that this statistic is not contradictory because inactive bass may suspend in either shade or in sunny open water. But for an angler to cast his lure near enough to inactive bass to make them strike, he usually must find bass that are suspended close to an identifiable target. While the target creates shade, the shade itself is incidental and not the reason the bass are there.

Another concern of bass anglers is that bass will move when they hear noise coming from above them. Manns’ study, however, clearly showed that bass learn to ignore any sounds that don’t represent a threat. Bass frequently swam beneath docks where workers were pounding with hammers or sailors were stomping in boats. Bass also ignored outboard motor noises in Lake Travis, which is heavily used by recreational boaters. Surprisingly, however, some of the lake’s bass had learned to move away from boats with electric fishing motors and it was difficult to approach such bass unless they were pushed against cover objects.

Manns suggests that a great myth of angling literature is that bass seek out preferred temperatures, water clarity, structure and so on. He stressed that the part of a bass’ brain that processes every piece of visual or physical information is about the size of a thumbnail. A bass simply does not have the ability to be intuitive.

“A bass will not run across a lake to an area that has a better temperature level,” Manns claimed, “because it doesn’t know it’s there. Now if a bass is wandering around and finds a more comfortable area, it might think, ‘Wow! Isn’t this temperature nice?’ And if it can stay there without getting hungry, it may hang around long enough for the angler with a temperature meter to catch it.”

In Manns’ study, bass did not move to deeper water to avoid surface temperatures in the 84° to 88° range. In fact, when all other factors were equal, black bass (including smallmouths) preferred the water temperature to be between 78° and 86°, not the 72° that is popularized in most fishing literature. Manns’ findings are corroborated by at least 20 other scientific studies published during a 40-year period. Bass found in cooler water are there because of a better food supply or because they are unable to compete with larger predators present in warmer water.

Manns admits that there are optimum chemical environments for bass but in most lakes the fish must be satisfied with less. They merely move into someplace tolerable, adapt to it and once they’ve adapted to that condition, any change is a negative change to those fish.

“Most people don’t realize that a bass is constantly adapting to its environment,” Manns declared. “The chemistry of its body adapts to temperature and water conditions and, if any factors change, the fish is temporarily out of balance. When cold fronts go through, for example, sudden drops in temperature disturb the fish’s equilibrium and it becomes slightly sick until it adapts to the new condition. That helps explain why bass normally don’t bite as well after a cold front.”

A common dream of anglers is catching bass on almost every cast from a “hotspot” where hungry largemouths eagerly await the next cast. The nearest thing I ever found to that fabled “hotspot” was on Lake Sam Rayburn back in 1968. My partner and I found a timbered flat near a river channel where every cast with our crankbaits hooked a chunky bass. We tied the boat line to a limb and promptly caught 60 largemouth bass from an area no larger than the average living room. In that era of 15-bass limits it was the first chance I had to cull bass to improve a stringer.

Our hopes for a repeat performance the next day were short lived. Our first few casts hooked bass, but the action ceased and we never relocated the bass. Nor did any of our friends ever find fish there on many subsequent trips. Manns offered an explanation for the phenomenon.

“They were probably transient bass, which is a major cause of confusion to anglers. Bass are generally thought of as having one type of behavior pattern. Actually, though, there are two types of black bass: transient bass and home-range bass. The concept is an old one that shows up in scientific literature as far back as 30 years ago.”

Manns theorized that the fish’s health or its ability to catch prey determines whether it is a home-range or transient bass. Healthy fish establish territories and hold them, moving as little as possible. They conserve energy by feeding quickly and remain in the same general location unless there is a major change in water conditions or the food supply.

Large bass populations or shortages of prey create competition for food, however. Those bass that feed less efficiently must feed longer and move often to find forage. A transient is one because it is a loser.

There are many transient bass in our better bass lakes and they almost always school. An angler who finds such a concentration can catch great numbers of them at one time but he should not necessarily label the area as a great fishing hole. Such catches can be the result of chance meetings with transients.

Manns says that an angler should not spend much time returning to the site of a big catch unless he has reason to believe, such as repeated success, that the place is a home-range area. An angler can more consistently catch bass by returning to locations where he has taken one or two fish over and over again. Those are probably spots that have enough food to hold home-range fish day after day.

Manns stressed that bass reactions vary according to how hungry they are. A very hungry fish will probably strike at any lure it can get in its mouth. A bass that has been feeding on shad for 20 minutes, however, will probably only take something that is similar to the shad.

Although bass can see colors, anglers should remember that bass do not perceive them as a human would. Manns recalled a day when he and his partner followed a large school of bass feeding heavily on shad. The two cast all of the traditional shad-color lures without a strike. As soon as Manns put on a gold lure, though, he caught fish on almost every cast.

The bass obviously responded to some flash of gold from the shad caused, perhaps, by the angle of sunlight. The bass had enough color discrimination that they triggered to the tiny variation. Manns, therefore, suggests that anglers who are unsuccessful in catching obviously active bass should change lure colors, sizes and actions until they find one that triggers the fish. Bass are not ambush predators as are halibut, angler fish and sculpin; nor are they chase predators such as tuna or striped bass. A black bass is a compromise fish designed to do a lot of things—none of them perfectly but all of them well. The bass is a seeker with a short sprint. It enters cover for protection and to rest, not to ambush prey. Cover actually limits and hinders a bass’ ability to strike and that is why prey fish hide in such areas.

The standard hunting pattern of a bass is to move around structure objects to see if anything strays far enough from cover to become vulnerable. It assesses its ability to catch individuals of prey and strikes only at those it thinks can be caught. The bass ignores prey that are likely to dodge and thus escape. A bass will ignore a lure that looks like a healthy bluegill just as it would ignore a real bluegill under the same circumstances.

“Bass fishing is very mathematical, very predictable,” Manns claimed. “It is a problem to be solved. Most of the false concepts arise because writers intellectualize about bass. The reaction mechanism of bass is actually very very basic: a reaction to a stimulus and a response, nothing more.

Read Next: Old-School Tips for Catching Big Bass Right Now

“A bass can’t outsmart you. All you have to do is find something that will stimulate it, put it where it will stimulate it and you will catch a bass. There’s really nothing more to fishing than that.”

Manns makes it sound simple and it looks simple enough when you watch him in action. He merely works the odds, covering as much favorable water as possible with a wide variety of lures. But he seems to catch many more bass than the average fisherman. The years he spent in their living quarters are paying off.

#8324. Lucky Skier Survives Night in Crevasse With Two Broken Legs

Thirty-nine-year-old Emeric Ossatte was ski touring alone earlier this week in the Savoie area of the French Alps, near the Italian border. He removed his skis to posthole up a steep couloir in his boots and crampons when an avalanche swept him downhill, depositing him into a crevasse and breaking both his legs.

The Grenoble-area man, who wasn’t carrying a beacon or radio, doffed his backpack and crawled 100m through the crevasse, looking for a way out. But the sides of the crack were too steep, and there was no cell service where he was. He remained beside an air pocket in the crevasse overnight. Meanwhile, when he didn’t check in that evening, his family raised the alarm.

According to a Facebook report by France’s CRS Savoie mountain-rescue unit, they saw the slide path but no immediate sign of Ossatte. The search continued until the middle of the night, when avalanche risk forced the rescuers to suspend their operations.

The following morning, a large contingent of 28 ground rescuers, dogs, three helicopters, and a physician to tend to Ossatte, if they found him alive, converged on the site, according to France 3. They found his gear and dug for five hours until they heard him calling out.

“He was really lucky,” said Major Yoann Haberey of the mountain-rescue team. “We knew he was under there. But we didn’t think we’d find him alive.”

By the time they extracted him, he had been there almost a day and a half. The hypothermic but lucky survivor was flown to the hospital for treatment and surgery on one of his legs.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

#8322. ‘8 Different Hunters Had Been After It.’ Giant Nontypical Buck Taken in Indiana

Published Nov 10, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Joe Merritt, age 41, of Middletown, Indiana had some target bucks he was after the first week of November during the state archery deer season. This is the first year he had cell cameras out to monitor multiple areas, and they proved to be a critical tool for taking the buck of a lifetime.

“On Tuesday, November 3 I got a trail camera photo of a huge buck sent to my cell phone from a very small woodlot that was my least favorite place I had to hunt,” Merritt tells Outdoor Life (he did the interview after just spending a morning hunting rabbits with his beagles). “The photos were of a huge buck that I’d never seen anywhere before.”

The next day the same cell phone camera got photos of the buck again. And the next morning, Nov. 5 at 3:55 a.m. he got photos of the huge buck again from the same camera in the same small wooded area.

“I knew I had to go after that buck right then,” says Merritt, who owns a coffee distribution company. “I left immediately and was in my saddle hanging from a  thin tree well before dawn.”

Merritt listened to deer in the woods around him long before daybreak. He wanted to be in his stand early to not disturb deer. He believes the spot was a bedding area near a sanctuary of private property where no hunting is allowed.

As an overcast dawn broke, he had a small 8-point buck come by his stand. Almost an hour later a doe showed.

“She kept looking behind her as she moved my way,” says Merritt. “She kept coming to me and flopped down and bedded just 35 yards away. The whole time she was looking back, and I was a little puzzled until the giant buck I had on camera showed up.”

But the giant buck moved toward the doe and bedded down near her, just 37 yards away.

“That’s when the chess game began,” says Merritt, who is also a dedicated houndsman who’s pursued raccoons and bears with dogs. “I was hanging from a very small diameter tree, and both deer were bedded close, and there was no chance for a bow shot while they were lying down.”

Merritt sat in his stand, quiet and still, for four hours before the doe finally got restless, stood, and walked toward Merritt. She was feeding on browse as she moved his way.

“That’s when the buck stood up, and started following her,” Merritt says. “The doe was right underneath me watching back at the buck when he turned and gave me a full broadside shot at 22 yards.”

Merritt made a perfect shot on the biggest deer of his life.

“I saw him running and blood was blowing out everywhere,” said the bowhunter. “I didn’t see or hear him fall. But I knew the shot was perfect and the buck would be easy to find.”

The doe was still feeding under his stand after Merritt shot and the buck ran.

“I just broke down in the stand, shaking and talking to myself about what just happened,” he says. “It took about 10 minutes for me to de-escalate and comprehend what had just happened.”

Then he climbed down to the ground and walked to where he’d last seen the buck. He walked right to the downed animal that was only 60 yards from where his arrow had struck it.

“I walked up to him and placed my hands on his rack and thanked the Lord for what had just happened,” says Merritt. “Then I phoned my wife and friends.”

Shortly, his close pal Jared Leppink joined Merritt in the woods and the two of them loaded the buck into an UTV. Then they shot photos of it, dressed the deer, and caped it.

While the buck has a massive rack, Merritt said the deer only weighed about 200 pounds – not a big animal for east-central Indiana, which regularly gives up 250 to 300-pound whitetails.

“I don’t think the buck was that old,” Merritt says. “The teeth were not very worn. I think he was between 3.5 to 5.5 years old.”

When pictures of his buck hit social media it created a wave of folks contacting him.

Read Next: Indiana Is Now the Top Trophy Whitetail State. Here’s Why

“I had to turn off my phone for 24 hours,” he says. “About eight different hunters had been after that buck and showed me photos of it. Some had shed horns from the buck from prior years. We figured I shot my buck 12 miles away from one hunter who thanked me for shooting the deer.

“I asked him why he was thanking me. He said, ‘Well now I know you got him, and I don’t have to keep passing other good bucks waiting for the 23-pointer to show up.’”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

#8320. Weekend Warm-Up: Behind the Climb

As the title suggests, Behind the Climb brings viewers behind the scenes with Colin Haley and Tyler Karow as they take on the Southeast Ridge of Cerro Torre. Colin Haley has tried the route, which he calls a modern classic, three times already without success. He hopes that by teaming up with Tyler Karow, the fourth time will be the charm.

As the short film opens, Haley introduces us to the route in voiceover, while on-screen, he and Karow make the approach, crossing ice fields and fording streams. The route is divided into three main sections: the Haston Crack, Ice Towers, and Headwall. Haley was turned back twice by bad weather on the Ice Towers; neither of them has finished the Headwall.

The Headwall section of Cerro Torre. Photo: Screenshot

Hoping to catch the Ice Towers still frozen and the Headwall still warmed by the sun, they wake up so early that the sun hasn’t actually finished setting yet. At that point, it seems more accurate to say that they took an evening nap and then climbed through the night.

Interspersed with clips of climbing, Haley and Karow review the footage, sharing the little logistical details and impressions of the climb. The Ice Towers, which Karow recalls attempting many years ago, have changed since he last encountered them. Once, as the name implies, thick with ice, much of it has now melted. “In another ten years,” Karow predicts, they will “look drastically different.”

Ice or no, they complete the second section. This time, the weather cooperates. Haley takes over for the final section, the dastardly Headwall which has repeatedly eluded him. The climbing itself is going well, but melting snow above is making the rock run with water. This, he admits, is a significant disadvantage to their carefully timed plan.

Despite the wet holds, Haley makes good progress. With only a few pitches to go, the self-described pessimist Haley admits that he finally thinks they’ll make it to the top. When he does finally reach the summit, grinning widely at the camera, voiceover reveals he has already started worrying about the descent.

Keeping with the promise to go behind the scenes, the video doesn’t stop at the summit, where they stand victoriously and admire the views. We follow the pair as they rappel down.

At the bottom, when asked how they feel about the adventure, both men reply simply that their hands hurt. Not all of us can be poets.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.
