#8318. The Gun Industry Is Divided Over Federal’s New High-Pressure 6.5 Creedmoor Ammo. Here’s Why

Published Nov 10, 2026 1:00 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

Late last month Bergara became the first gun manufacturer to explicitly state Federal’s 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak ammo is not approved for use in its bolt-action rifles. This reverses earlier guidance that the new high-pressure cartridge technology was compatible with Bergara’s existing 6.5 Creedmoor rifles.

The 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak, introduced this summer, has the same dimensions as a traditional brass 6.5 Creedmoor cartridge and was specifically designed by Federal Ammunition for use in existing 6.5 Creed rifles. It is not a new cartridge, but an improvement to an existing cartridge. This unusual dynamic is part of the regulatory and potential liability concerns currently vexing the gun industry.

Federal’s proprietary steel-alloy case technology contains higher pressures that drive bullets at faster speeds for better performance. Plus Peak operates at 80,000 psi, compared to about 62,000 to 65,000 psi for brass 6.5 Creed cartridges. This is possible because the Peak Alloy case material that Federal has developed is much stronger than brass. It acts as a containment vessel insulating the action and barrel from the extra pressure without inflicting extra stress on the rifle.

Related: They Built a Rifle Cartridge That Could Change Hunting and Shooting Forever

The ammunition has undergone substantial testing by Federal, a dozen gunmakers and component companies, the U.S. military (which has ordered 40 million cases), and other industry professionals.

And indeed, Bergara rifles were originally deemed “compatible” with this 6.5 Creed Plus Peak ammo, according to previous guidance from Federal and media coverage of the ammo’s launch in June. But on Oct. 29 Bergara announced that the new ammunition “is NOT approved for use in any Bergara rifle chambered in 6.5 Creedmoor.”

“These cartridge pressures haven’t been evaluated under SAAMI’s standard development process,” reads the statement, referring to the Sporting Arms and Ammunition Manufacturing Institute. “Ammunition operating outside SAAMI pressure standards for its caliber should not be used in any rifle unless Bergara formally confirms compatibility for that model.”

SAAMI sets the gun industry’s self-imposed guardrails with voluntary performance standards for firearms, ammunition, and suppressors. Bergara did not cite any incidents of catastrophic failures or injury related to shooting the new case technology through its rifles. (No one we spoke to for this story reported being aware of a catastrophic failure while testing the Plus Peak in existing 6.5 Creed rifles.)

Bergara announced in 2025 it would launch rifles in 7mm Backcountry, which debuted the same high-pressure cartridge technology as the Plus Peak. Various online distributors and retailers indicate Bergara’s HMR Pro and MG Lite models would be available in the 7BC, but the company doesn’t currently offer any rifles chambered in 7BC on its website. It’s unclear if any preproduction or production models were ever manufactured. Bergara declined to comment for this story.

In a June statement, SAAMI acknowledged the development of the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak and the creation of a working group — at Federal’s request — to evaluate the new case technology on “performance, pressure, testing, and interchangeability standards.”

SAAMI and its member companies are also discussing “the technical issues and opportunities presented by advanced cartridge case technologies and their interaction with existing firearms and suppressors.”

The organization stopped short of taking a position on the ammunition and instead urged hunters and shooters to contact their rifle’s manufacturer before shooting Federal’s 6.5 Creed Plus Peak.

“At this time, SAAMI’s guidance stays the same,” SAAMI’s senior director of technical affairs, Alan Serven, tells Outdoor Life. “As this is not a standardized cartridge, we can’t really say anything definitive about it, so we are directing folks to the manufacturer of their firearm. And we do caution that an answer from one manufacturer cannot be interpreted as a blanket statement … It’s on a case-by-case basis with each of the manufacturers and potentially down to model.”

On Nov. 5 the sporting-goods retailer Scheels sent an internal memo to its employees announcing any Plus Peak ammo should not be stocked on store floors or made available to customers.

“As of yesterday morning we have added a ‘DNS’ Do not Stock rating to all SKUs of Federal 6.5 Creedmoor +Peak ammunition,” reads the memo, obtained by Outdoor Life. “The Kinetic Group [Federal’s parent company], gun manufacturers and SAAMI are continuing to review this product. Once we have more information on their review and the stocking of the 6.5 Creedmoor +Peak, we will make a determination on how to proceed.”

Scheels has put all direct orders for Plus Peak on hold, according to the memo. The retailer also instructed its ammo and gun teams to accept any returns from customers and put any received Plus Peak “somewhere where we can ensure it will not make its way to the floor to be sold.” When Outdoor Life called a Scheels store asking about Plus Peak availability and the possibility of ordering any, the employee who answered the phone replied that no, this was not possible because “corporate put the kibosh on this until SAAMI figures out the safety and pressure issues.” The store policy affects all five SKUs of Plus Peak.

“Federal’s Peak Alloy case technology is the wave of the future for rifle ammunition,” Kinetic Group’s VP of marketing Jason Nash tells Outdoor Life, noting that Federal sought to work with as many rifle makers as possible in its development of high-pressure loads. “Over the past seven years Federal has manufactured millions of rounds in multiple cartridges and firearms. Federal has the utmost confidence in this new technology and its function in today’s firearms.”

Some gunmakers have confirmed to OL that all their existing 6.5 Creedmoor rifles are compatible with Plus Peak and they have no safety concerns whatsoever. Others have remained quiet on the advisability of using Plus Peak in their 6.5 Creedmoor rifles. Bergara remains the only rifle maker who has publicly disallowed its use in its bolt-action rifles. (Break-action rifles from CVA, Bergara’s sister company, are also not approved for Plus Peak, according to Federal’s site.)

This about-face on the use of Plus Peak in Bergara rifles raises many more questions than it answers. For instance, is this hesitancy around the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak rooted in legitimate concerns of consumer safety? Or is it ultimately driven by the potential threat that some companies see to their bottom lines? Here’s what to know.

Although the conversations around Plus Peak in SAAMI’s working group are confidential, industry insiders are keyed into the crux of the issue. Here are two of the primary camps.

No one seems to be disputing the performance gains of the Plus Peak ammo. Instead, the debate is over how to standardize it.

“It definitely is an interesting idea what Federal is doing,” says one gun manufacturer who spoke on the condition of anonymity. “The industry itself is not politicking against it. The industry is not against new technology. We know and understand new products are what drive business. That’s not the issue. There’s probably some ammunition companies that are bitter or angry they didn’t think of it first, but that’s also neither here nor there.”

According to this source, the problem is the new technology currently puts too much onus on gunmakers without the opportunity to earn revenue. In fact, it poses a big expense for bigger manufacturers.

“For those of us who played by the rules and built guns to SAAMI’s standards, now we have an ammunition out there that’s outside that spec. And we’re at a conundrum to say, ‘Is it safe or not safe in our guns,’” says the gunmaker. “So one [option is] we have to go back and retest all our guns at an immense cost to verify those guns will pass these pressures.”

Some guns will inevitably pass, they say, but other guns may not. This could result in a laundry list of liability concerns in which gun companies must painstakingly note — online and in owners manuals — which rifle models are compatible and incompatible with Plus Peak.

The biggest fear is that a customer will get hurt. It’s also a bad look for any existing guns that don’t pass, given that it “may expose ‘weaknesses’ that don’t exist” in rifles that were built to spec for standard 6.5 Creed loads.

Another option for gunmakers is to simply not retest guns and not comment on (or in Bergara’s case, publicly come out against) the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak.

“It does kind of stand in the face of why we have SAAMI and why we self regulate,” says the source. “So it’s a legitimate concern. We won’t approve [Plus Peak] either. Will our guns pass it? Most likely. But we’re not chomping at the bit to go through all the testing to verify that. We have other things we need to worry about.”

The right way to have handled this new ammo technology, in this gunmaker’s mind, was to have rolled out a new cartridge that could have more easily received SAAMI approval and allowed gunmakers to collaborate on new rifle builds.

“We would have all happily engaged in a new caliber that did this, like the 7 Backcountry. We knew the pressures, and we could build a gun specifically to handle that caliber with those higher pressures. No problem. It’s the retro-fitting for everybody. Why should we go to a pile of work? Just build a new caliber. It’s a very cool technology. We’re all about that. We’re not dragging our feet because we’re old and crotchety. We’re trying to protect our business and this industry.”

In that scenario this ammunition wouldn’t be a 6.5 Creedmoor using Plus Peak technology — it would be a whole new cartridge called, for instance, the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak. Of course, this would require shooters to buy a new rifle.

“The difference is meaningful,” says Outdoor Life shooting editor John Snow of this hypothetical scenario. “And presumably, you’d have to have firearms marked with that new cartridge name in order to be in compliance with what SAAMI has deemed acceptable.”

Federal has treated the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak not as a new cartridge, but an improvement on existing 6.5 Creed ammo. The difference, again, is that it contains higher-than-SAAMI-approved pressures that drive bullets at higher velocities.

These souped-up loads give hunters and shooters the ability to get 6.5 PRC-like performance out of their existing 6.5 Creedmoor rifles.

To some, the nature of Plus Peak is so monumental that industry disruption is natural and even necessary.

“We are embracing high-pressure technology like Peak Alloy,” says Sean Murphy, director of sales and marketing for Proof Research (and an occasional OL contributor). “It’s a development similar to switching from black to smokeless powder.”

Some of the more nimble gunmakers, like Proof, have already tackled the testing they need to feel comfortable about giving customers approval to run the higher-pressure load in its guns. That’s because Proof predicts this high-pressure ammunition is going to be a major player in the future, and the company is working to develop barrel and rifle technology to complement it by maximizing accuracy and performance.

“Proof Research has extensively tested Federal Peak Alloy cases in multiple cartridges, including 6.5 Creedmoor and 7 Backcountry,” says Murphy. “In 6.5 Creedmoor alone, thousands of rounds have been fired through hunting, target, and semi-automatic precision rifles with no case failures or firearm damage. Additionally, many thousands of rounds have been fired by Proof with Peak Alloy 5.56 ammunition, also without issue … Chamber-bore obstruction testing across every Proof Research receiver design showed Peak Alloy cases outperforming equivalent brass cases, with a greater safety margin at 80,000 psi than brass cases at 60,000 psi.”

The Montana-based rifle maker is also one of the most advanced barrel makers in the country. It spent years developing its PXT barrel, which Proof says accomodates much higher pressures and velocities without shortening the barrel’s lifespan.

“During our PXT barrel testing and development, more than 10,000 rounds of 7 Backcountry were fired — including 5,000 and 3,500 on individual receivers — with no damage, and wear levels comparable to brass-cased ammunition,” says Murphy.

The company also issued an apparent rebuttal to Bergara’s announcement of Oct. 29 with posts to Instagram and Facebook that same day, declaring full support and formal endorsement of Peak Alloy cartridges in its rifles.

“We have tested all our rifles — and many from other manufacturers — extensively with the Plus Peak ammunition and it’s proven to be completely safe,” says Murphy.

As part of its development and testing of the Plus Peak, Federal worked with a dozen gunmakers and related businesses. Federal confirmed to American Rifleman this summer that certain rifles from five of those companies are compatible with Plus Peak: Christensen Arms, Weatherby, Seekins Precision, Geissele, and Nemo Arms. Federal matches guidance from SAAMI that hunters and shooters should contact their gunmaker to specify which models are approved.

“Our testing has shown 6.5 Creedmoor +Peak operates in a variety of 6.5 Creedmoor firearm platforms,” Federal advises in the Plus Peak guide on its website. “Refer to your rifle manufacturer for recommendations on compatibility with high-pressure ammunition.”

Christensen Arms echoes similar language on its website. However, all existing Christensen Arms rifles chambered in 6.5 Creedmoor are compatible with the Plus Peak ammo, a spokesperson confirmed to Outdoor Life. The additional seven brands who worked with Federal include Area 419, Forster Products, Proof Research, Pure Precision, Hill Country Rifles, Short Action Customs, and Springfield Armory.

SAAMI has good cause to be extra cautious, points out Snow, since it’s a voluntary organization. The gun industry has regulated itself for a century — the result of a handshake deal with the government after World War I — and would like to continue avoiding federal oversight “like that of the auto industry,” says Snow.

But one reason gunmakers may come out against Plus Peak in their rifles has nothing to do with safety, cost, or liability.

“It’s possible that companies could see poor accuracy with the Plus Peak ammo and not recommend it,” says another industry insider, who also spoke on the condition of anonymity. “High chamber pressures, especially with some barrels and rifling designs, can cause accuracy problems that aren’t safety issues.”

Federal has patented its high-pressure cartridge technology, which means other ammunition makers can’t leverage it without paying for the privilege.

“There are rival companies that would arguably like to see this new and innovative technology put into a box and shoved off to the side,” says Snow. “Because they don’t have the ability to capitalize on this trend currently, and it’s potentially bad for their business if Plus Peak succeeds.”

The Peak Alloy case technology used in the 6.5 Creed Plus Peak was pioneered in the relatively new 7mm Backcountry, which debuted in January 2025 and was rooted in development of high-pressure ammunition for the military.

When the 7mm Backcountry was introduced, we tested more of it than anyone (besides Federal’s engineers) by shooting every SKU and running it in a dozen rifles. Although the 7BC delivered on its promise of velocity and produced accuracy equivalent to factory ammo in other cartridges, we encountered one major problem with almost every rifle. After 40 to 120 rounds, the 7BC rifles developed very stiff primary and secondary extraction. It turned out this was a common issue for other testers and shooters, too.

This issue, ultimately, was rooted in the case dimensions of the cartridge — not poorly cut chambers, as some people have speculated. After receiving updated 7BC ammo and testing the new batches in rifles that were previously showing these issues, we’ve found that the extraction issues have been resolved.

Those initial extraction issues in the 7BC were — erroneously — interpreted as effects of the increased pressure of the cartridge on the guns themselves. That doesn’t appear to be the case. In reality, most rifles chambered in 7mm Backcountry aren’t constructed differently from similar models designed for brass-cased cartridges like the 6.5 Creed.

So in other words, concerns over pressure issues from the new cartridge materials from the 7mm Backcountry to the 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak appear largely unfounded.

The 7 Backcountry was accepted by SAAMI without the current speed bumps, in part, because it’s an entirely new cartridge. It required brand-new guns manufactured specifically for the cartridge. Gunmakers could either opt to produce a gun chambered in 7BC — or not. Federal’s choice to offer the Plus Peak in an existing cartridge was a different route, but a calculated one.

“The reason Federal went with the 6.5 Creedmoor is because the 6.5 Creed was introduced in 2007 and is only available in modern firearms that are of recent vintage,” says Snow. “So it is extraordinarily unlikely, I would almost say impossible, that we would see, for example, a Plus Peak .308 or .223 offered commercially. That’s because there are way too many actions and rifles of older vintages that cannot be vouched for.”

Serven could not provide a timeline on when SAAMI’s Plus Peak working group might disclose its findings.

“Our primary mission is safety, so we are proceeding with that in mind. That concern is the overriding factor,” says Serven. “So we are not in a hurry. The priority is that we get the right answer, not a fast answer.”

SAAMI’s acceptance of new cartridges standardizes the case and load across ammo makers. The organization’s blessing is not required for the sale and use of ammunition, and many niches of the hunting, shooting, and military world operate outside the bounds of SAAMI approval. This includes everyone from brands like Buffalo Bore Ammunition to wildcat cartridge developers to the federal government itself. It also means you can still get your hands on 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak.

Federal currently offers the Terminal Ascent Plus Peak for sale on its site at a limit of two boxes per customer. The four other loads are “currently unavailable.” Plenty of YouTubers have been able to get their hands on the ammo, and none we’ve seen have reported safety issues.

“The stakes are pretty consequential,” says Snow of how SAAMI decides to handle the Plus Peak ammo. “On the one hand, are we going to move with less friction and resistance into the era of high-pressure, high-performance cartridges? Or are there going to be speed bumps and other impediments put in place that effectively throttle or discourage the adoption of this new technology?”

Read Next: I Shot the First Elk With the New 6.5 Creedmoor Plus Peak Ammo. It’s an Undeniable Elk Hunting Cartridge

The push to adopt high-pressure rounds has come from the U.S. military, which has mandated it to maintain an advantage over “near peer” adversaries (such as Russia and China) on the battlefield. The military wants rifles capable of defeating newer body armor, and the way to do that is to drive bullets from existing guns at greater speeds.

“The government has been crystal clear about what they want and expect,” says Snow, “which is that high-pressure cartridges are happening and need to be here sooner rather than later.”

Natalie Krebs is the Executive Editor of Outdoor Life, where she tackles everything from reporting digital features to producing podcast episodes. Originally from Missouri, she currently lives in northwest Arkansas with her bird dog, Hatchet.

Tyler Freel is a Staff Writer for Outdoor Life. He lives in Fairbanks, Alaska and has been covering a variety of topics for OL for more than a decade. From backpack sheep hunting adventure stories to DIY tips to gear and gun reviews, he covers it all with a perspective that’s based in experience.

#8315. Kayaking 3,700km Around Britain

Dominic Gribbin, 61, is more than five months into his attempt to circumnavigate mainland Great Britain by sea kayak. But relentless weather delays and mounting costs have made the journey even more challenging than anticipated.

Gribbin launched his expedition from Crinan Harbour in Argyll, Scotland, on June 2. Paddling clockwise, he has covered 2,584km. From Crinan, he continued up the west coast of Scotland and around Cape Wrath and Duncansby Head before heading south along the eastern and southern coasts. His latest reported landing point is Cliff End, near Hastings, Sussex.

The journey has not gone according to plan. Persistent Atlantic weather systems have cost him weeks of paddling time, while the financial demands of keeping the expedition moving have forced him to return to freelance television camerawork so he and his wife, Nicola, can keep it running.

While he paddles, she follows him in their motor home, offering shore support. He now hopes to complete the circle at Crinan in the spring of 2027. ExplorersWeb caught up with him to find out more about the journey. This interview has been edited for brevity and clarity.

How do you approach the daily distances on a journey like this?

Given that circumnavigating the British mainland is at least 3,700km, I have aimed to do at least 40km per day. My longest day so far has been 64km. However, weather, tides and daylight length, particularly in autumn and winter, often curtail one’s best-laid plans. Some days have been necessarily shorter, forcing me to make an earlier-than-envisaged retreat to shore.

But this is very much a journey, not a race, so we will continue into the spring of 2027. It’s an incredible opportunity to explore every nook and cranny of the GB coastline.

How much has the weather affected your progress?

Roughly 85% of delays have been due to poor weather, 5% to Ministry of Defense firing range timetable restrictions, and 10% to the need for very specific tidal windows to navigate particular sections safely and efficiently. I have never had a delay due to fatigue or the need for a break from the sheer physical nature of this endurance challenge. I have used days when I have inevitably been “weathered-off” to recoup my energy reserves.

For most of the trip, it has been extremely rare to have more than four days of consistently good paddling weather. Setting off from Crinan on June 2 meant we did not enjoy the heat wave experienced in southern Britain. Instead, we lost at least six weeks to poor weather. Constant Atlantic westerly and northwesterly fronts brought wind, rain, and high seas that seemed to permanently batter northwest and northern Scotland throughout June, July, and even into early August. It was one of the great untalked-about weather phenomena of summer 2026. It wasn’t all heat waves…at least not in Scotland!

Are you camping or sleeping in the motorhome?

Northwest Scotland and its wild, deeply indented coastline often made road access to the sea impossible. Early on, we accepted that there would be long stretches when Nicola would not see me for over 24 hours. She was often doing double the mileage I was doing and negotiating some very hairy driving conditions on roads way off the beaten track. That resulted in many planned and unplanned wild camps along the coast.

By the time we reached the north coast of Scotland, road access had improved dramatically, and Nicola and I were generally able to coordinate to meet at the end of each day. This has proved an invaluable motivator, especially when the going gets tough. Our little motorhome has been a blessed retreat each night since then: a warm, reassuring space to relax, gather one’s thoughts, and plan ahead.

The route so far. The blue line indicates the paddling route, the red line shows the route taken by the support vehicle. Image: Dominic Gribbin

What kayak are you paddling, and how much equipment do you carry?

I am paddling around Britain in a Rockpool Taran 16 sea kayak. It is a fast, beautifully handmade sea kayak, perfect for putting down the miles and handling heavy seas. The trade-off is stability, but it has a prodigious load capacity in its various watertight bulkheads.

I carry a kayak trolley permanently on the back deck. I have already worn out two and am constantly experimenting with new designs to better negotiate the challenges of gravel and sand beaches. As a minimum, I carry basic wild-camping equipment in case I need to spend an unexpected night ashore.

Food consists of pre-cooked rice bags, canned fish and vegetables, alongside plenty of snacks. I favor savory rather than sweet food. Coffee is my essential luxury, and I normally carry a can or two of beer!

Safety equipment is extensive. I carry a Garmin 86i Marine GPS with InReach satellite tracking, an ICOM marine handheld VHF radio with AIS and GPS, a spare VHF radio, flares and smoke signals, and a McMurdo personal locator beacon. I also carry a fully integrated Garmin Fenix 7 Pro watch, first-aid kit, spare paddles, compass, mobile phone, battery packs, head lamp, repair tape, and a Swiss Army penknife.

The kayak itself weighs 24.9kg, but the overall weight, including my dry suit, buoyancy aid, clothing, and equipment, easily reaches 35kg or more. It is a significant challenge to negotiate all this weight over an often-unfriendly shoreline. However, the kayak benefits from the additional weight when afloat.

What have been the most memorable moments along the way?

We have had numerous encounters with sea otters, hunting pods of bottlenose dolphins, and close-up encounters with bottlenose mothers and calves. There have also been golden eagles and white-tailed sea eagles, the largest raptor in the UK. Some of the most sublime moments have been the inshore sea passages through the sheltered waters of the Inner Hebrides.

Successfully rounding Cape Wrath was a truly intimidating but awe-inspiring milestone. Then there was rounding the infamous Duncansby Head and crossing the Pentland Firth near John O’Groats, finally seeing the compass turn south. The huge breeding seabird colonies off Caithness and much of Scotland’s northeast coast have been incredible. Literally tens of thousands of birds, a deafening cacophony of noise!

Right along the GB coastline, we have also met some of the warmest and most generous people.

Have there been any particularly frightening moments?

The huge seas off Strathy Point, Caithness, are probably the closest call yet. If something had gone wrong there while two kilometers offshore in those seas, I wouldn’t be writing this.

I have experienced plenty of rough and scary seas so far, but that occasion was another order of magnitude. They were enormous seas driven by Arctic swell and spring tides. They were the biggest seas I have ever paddled in. I am not in a rush to return there, despite its beauty.

You have mentioned that you are now combining freelance work with the expedition. How are you managing the financial and logistical side?

We were able to make this happen because I am self-employed. We bankrolled the expedition using our savings. The motorhome for shore support was the biggest investment. We initially calculated finances based on finishing by mid-autumn, but those weather delays cost us dearly. We ran out of money by mid-September, so I have had to return to my freelance work as a TV cameraman and juggle that with keeping this Edge of Britain expedition going until we are finished.

There is no stopping this winter. Instead, we will take advantage of every weather window.

I began paddling at age 11 when I permanently borrowed a friend’s old kayak and started exploring the ankle-deep waters of the River Ver and River Colne around St. Albans and Park Street, Hertfordshire. At 13, I started British Canoe Union training. This began my lifelong involvement with whitewater paddling on rivers around the world.

Back in the 1970s and 1980s, when I began, skills progression was funneled entirely through very actively encouraged participation in canoe slalom. Everybody who learned to paddle at club level was involved in slalom to some degree. I chose men’s C1 (Canadian singles) and competed at a national level, ranking 19th in Great Britain by the age of 18.

University changed my orientation somewhat, and the allure of traveling to exotic whitewater rivers around the world took over. Expedition whitewater paddling then became my life, and still is, alongside expedition sea kayaking. Combining this with filmmaking ultimately led directly to my career as a television cameraman.

Why did you decide to take on a circumnavigation of Britain?

The decision to attempt to paddle around the GB mainland is the culmination of a life spent kayaking. It is hard not to fall in love with the sheer beauty and allure of the world of the river and coastline, particularly here at home in the UK. This country has what is widely celebrated as some of the best sea kayaking in the world. To dream of exploring the whole GB coastline in as much detail as time and weather allow is, to me, a logical conclusion.

The actual decision came extremely late in the day, namely January 2026. This began a frantic period of organization ahead of our June 2 launch. Admittedly, April would have been better, but prior commitments meant we had to begin in June.

I chose a clockwise route around Britain, thinking (naively) that the prevailing winds would vaguely be behind us for at least half the journey. I also wanted to tackle the potentially most technically demanding section of the northwest and north coast of Scotland early, in the height of what I envisaged as long summer days. How wrong I was!

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

ExplorersWeb is part of AllGear Digital's portfolio of media brands.

#8314. A Bowhunt for Caribou Along Alaska’s Famed Haul Road Is Even Tougher Than It Sounds

Published Nov 10, 2026 2:58 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

We had to be getting close. I picked my way through the rocks along the edge of the half-frozen creek, knowing that any minute I would intersect trails beaten into the snow by thousands of caribou hooves. But could we make it in time? We couldn’t see the trails as we hurried alongside the steep cut bank, although I knew they had to be right here somewhere. I climbed up a few feet to peek over the bank, only to see a hundred caribou about 50 yards away. They saw me too. The stalk was blown.

The hundreds of caribou that we had tried to ambush were now moving away. Though we could hear the grunting, snorting, and occasional clash of antlers just over the hill, they might as well have been a mile away. They flowed like water over the tundra.

All we could do was look for another group of caribou to stalk. Luckily this shouldn’t be much of a problem, as thousands of caribou were migrating through this snowy stretch of rolling hills and wide valleys at the northern edge of the Brooks Range, within the largest bowhunting-only area on the continent.

The Dalton Highway, or “Haul Road” as it is commonly referred to, could be considered an irreconcilable dichotomy. It’s where ambitious industry and true wilderness meet—and coexist. The 414-mile industrial road was constructed in 1974 to facilitate building the Trans-Alaska Pipeline and service the new oilfields in Prudhoe Bay. It provides the vital link that allows oil to flow all the way to the Port of Valdez to this day.

Although its primary function is still to service the pipeline and oil fields, the Haul Road was opened to the public in 1980 and has provided and created access to millions of acres of public land. The Dalton Highway Corridor Management Area extends five miles on either side of the road and stretches about 370 miles from the Yukon River north to Prudhoe Bay, in which only bowhunting for big game is allowed.

“This is going to be a long drive,” I told my dad and our videographer Tanner Denton as we loaded up our gear with the snow still falling.

With about 500 miles to go and six inches of wet snow on the ground, we had a challenging drive ahead. The Haul Road is not only the most remote highway in the U.S., but it also has a reputation for tough driving conditions. It’s been featured on shows like Ice Road Truckers, and most rental vehicle contracts explicitly forbid driving on it. Even travel guides suggest bringing not one, but two new spare tires. For decades, the road was all gravel, and although some sections are now paved, much of the pavement is honestly worse than the gravel. On the drive, you’ll encounter huge potholes, frost heaves, rocks slung into your windshield from passing big rigs, and a unique silty sludge that will coat your entire vehicle. It was early October, and we knew that wet, muddy, and icy conditions were going to be a fact of life.

Despite the relatively easy access, popularity, and long seasons, I hadn’t ever really given an honest effort to a Haul Road hunt. I’m usually distracted by sheep and moose hunting, and aside from sneaking away on days off while working in Prudhoe Bay a couple times, the last time I had made the drive was in 2009 with my dad. So it was fitting that Dad was coming along with me on this trip.

Even so, this was anything but a slam dunk. As migrators, caribou are where they are, and you find them when you find them.

We hit the road shortly after first light. By the time the sun began to set, we were looking at more than a thousand caribou scattered across the barren hillsides in the northern foothills of the Brooks Range. With the temperature dropping quickly, it was time to set up camp, string our bows, and heat the wood stove. As we discussed our plan for the following morning, we hoped that the caribou tracks that littered the fresh snow around our camp were a good sign. I had been told by folks who are much more experienced than I, that this time of year, the herds of caribou will overnight up in the hills, then begin moving down in mid-morning. It looked like we were in a good position.

The next morning, we began gearing up as the coffee percolator thumped on the small wood stove. We periodically peeked outside the tent, looking for any major movement in the herds of caribou.

Our basic strategy was simple and dictated almost entirely by the terrain and the nature of the caribou. In herds of hundreds or more, on completely barren tundra, it would be all but impossible to sneak close enough for a shot with our recurves. When caribou did pass by terrain features that might allow a stalk, the herds were almost constantly moving. At a feeding, strolling pace, it would be impossible for us to keep up or gain ground on them to get into position. Instead, this hunt had to be an ambush. We had to take advantage of any terrain feature we could hide in, and let the caribou come to us.

“They’re pointed this direction,” I said as I stuck my head out of the tent. “I think we need to get moving.”

A herd of caribou was on its feet and moving on a low ridgeline about a mile away, angling for a ravine a few hundred yards from camp. Before we had much time to react, caribou began popping up onto the ridge on the other side of the creek, having already crossed. We had missed our opportunity.

“I think they’re going to drop down to the bottom eventually, so let’s work around and try to find a way to get in front of them,” I said.

We quickly hooked to our left, just above the base of the hills that the herd was moving along. They didn’t seem to be in a hurry. I don’t often think of caribou as an especially vocal animal, so it’s always surprising to hear their bellowing and snorting. As we crested the top of the hill which had been covered in caribou minutes earlier, we saw the last animals of the herd cresting over another skyline, headed farther back into the mountains. The barnyard-like scent of caribou hung in the air as we walked back.

When I saw the trails converge into one spot crossing the ravine above camp, I knew that this place had everything we needed for an ambush. The caribou had to cross a hilltop, then drop down a tall, steep bank, then cross two different channels before climbing out on a single trail that exited the other side. We would be able to see the next herd coming from camp, then quickly move into position to intercept them.

Back at camp, the percolator hadn’t even started boiling when another herd came over the far ridgeline, headed right for that creek. We threw on our backpacks and grabbed our bows, the herd moving closer with every precious second. The animals were coming from the north and I wanted to be on the south edge of the ravine. We would be able to hide and have plenty of time to see bulls coming and get ready. I didn’t cross the creek soon enough though, and it quickly turned to deep pools covered by unstable ice. Crossing now was too risky, so we continued moving upstream against the steep bank. Just as we had almost reached the trail that the caribou were headed toward, I looked over that cut bank and spookedthe entire herd. It was so close. If we had been just one or two minutes earlier, the outcome might have been different.

By that evening, the herds of caribou had abandoned their ridgetop route and began congregating in and moving through the valley below our camp. After rushing back and forth all day, we jumped in the truck and moved to a spot where we could see the entire valley. As evening began to fall, a big herd that had spent the day in the relative safety of the wide-open and flat valley began to move. Like spilled water trickling off a tabletop, a stream of caribou began to pull from the immense herd. In minutes, the line of caribou stretched as far as we could see into the mountains, crossing the creek just downhill of our camp. We tried one last time to slip into a gap in the herd, but that didn’t work either.

The next morning, we made the short drive from camp to where the road crested a nearby hill, giving us a good vantage of the whole valley. We could see hundreds (if not thousands) of caribou scattered across the foothills in the distance. They were beginning to move down to the valley floor. One cow would make the decision to go, followed by a single-file line of animals until the whole herd vanished. It was apparent that the whole mess of them was going to cross the road in a spot with virtually no cover and a howling 20 mph wind. To complicate matters, two other truckloads of hunters who were camped nearby were already positioning to try and have a crack at the approaching herd. We decided to wait where we were.

It was possible that some caribou could cross the creek we had been hunting, either above or below camp—we just needed to know which way they’d go.

As the caribou flowed across the road, we quickly turned around, parked the truck, and began walking down the creek to intercept them. They were crossing the gulley in a shallow, flat spot, several hundred yards from the edge of any brush. We managed to slip into a sizeable gap in the herd and were frantically trying to find a position to hide and wait, when my dad (who was kneeling right next to me) whispered, “There’s antlers right there, about 30 yards!”

If there’s one thing that’s a dead giveaway that a bull caribou is coming, it’s his set of tall antlers. If there’s any terrain between you, you’ll see his antlers long before he can see you.

If there’s one thing that’s a dead giveaway that a bull caribou is coming, it’s his set of tall antlers. If there’s any terrain between you, you’ll see his antlers long before he can see you. And at that moment, there were antlers sticking up above the small embankment—very close. We were in a bad spot though. I nocked an arrow, but Dad had to move away from me slightly. When he did that, the caribou spotted us and spooked, circling wide and crossing the shallow depression.

We cautiously stood and, not seeing any more caribou, tried to find a better position. Soon we spotted more antlers over a softly rolling rise in the tundra. Down on my knees, arrow nocked, I knew they were going to cross too far from us. In good conditions, 40 yards was pushing my reasonable limit with my recurve, but with a 20-mph wind, it was out of the question. One herd crossed at 75 yards, then another at 60, and then the herd fizzled out. After repositioning again and waiting, we decided to grab a cup of coffee and regroup. Each time we were getting closer, and we knew that something had to break our way sooner or later.

Before a much-needed lunch, I opted to drive back up to our vantage point and make sure nothing else was coming. Almost as soon as we got there, Dad and I saw yet another big herd. This group was on a trajectory that would take them near the line of tall brush that lined much of the creek, and we might have a place to hide. Again, we parked and hurried, walking through the brush as quickly as we could.

“We’re not going to make it in time,” I said, frustrated by our constant race against the clock.

I could see the backs of the caribou through the brush as they made their way across the open flat. They were just too fast, and we came to the end of the brush just as they started crossing the creek about 120 yards away.

“Why don’t you just try sneaking up there by yourself,” my dad said. “Three guys are too many, but one might be able to slip in there.”

It was worth a shot. Between us and the herd, the wash that the creek flowed through was about 3 to 4 feet deep, with a little bit of rolling terrain that might obscure the caribou that were coming. After a hundred or so had crossed, I began quickly, but carefully, moving down the draw. Eventually, some of the caribou that had already crossed began to see me, but kept on their way. I was still obscured from most of the animals that hadn’t yet crossed. I was skeptical about my chances, but after a small group saw me at about 60 yards, flared, and crossed farther down, a gap in the herd appeared. With antlers just coming into view, I quickly covered the remaining distance and dropped to my knees. I hid next to a small bush, arms-length from the main trail the herd had created. I watched the antlers, which looked to be following the group I had just bumped. There was nothing I could do but wait. I quickly ranged a rock in the cut bank across the winding trails in front of me. It was 30 yards away. I told myself I wouldn’t shoot at anything beyond that.

Seeing the rest of the herd safely across the creek must have calmed the caribou, because they quickly turned back toward me. All I could think as they closed the distance was: Don’t make eye contact.

Exposed, but holding absolutely still with my head tilted down, the cow that was leading the group came closer, followed by a nice bull. At under 10 yards I could hear her sniffing the air, even over the whipping wind. The bull was beyond her. As if I didn’t even exist, the cow walked past me, only paces away. My eyes shifted to the bull who was quartering to me, then to the rock I had ranged. He was only about 20-25 yards I figured, so I drew my bow.

I was in a state of calm disbelief. Somehow I accepted that, aside from the timing and placement of my arrow, the situation was totally out of my control. I expected the bull to come out of his skin as soon as I moved, but he didn’t even notice me until I hit full draw. His head turned toward me at some point, but I only remember checking off my shot process. Anchor, good alignment, aim, expand—and the arrow disappeared into the bull. I wanted to hit him through the meat of the front shoulder to fully catch both lungs, but my arrow went a little high and left. The bull crossed the creek in a sprint then turned back. I stood to watch him as he ran about 150 yards back to meet the confused herd. The second the bull stopped running, another big bull ran up, hooked his antlers, and threw my bull on the ground, thrashing him as if my bull owed him money.

After the herd moved off, I walked up to my bull, still in disbelief. This beautiful white-caped bull at my feet was the true embodiment of the Arctic. As a hunter, I always experience a sense of thankfulness and relief when I can finally put my hands on an animal that I’ve hunted, and I felt that with this bull.

But more than the thankfulness for possession, of meat, hide, antlers, there was something that I haven’t experienced so strongly in a long time. It was a raw appreciation of that moment, that every hunter feels right when it works out. The feeling transcends space and time and connects the hunter to the feeling of the arrow being released. That experience is a true gift that will last long after all the meat is gone.

Tyler Freel is a Staff Writer for Outdoor Life. He lives in Fairbanks, Alaska and has been covering a variety of topics for OL for more than a decade. From backpack sheep hunting adventure stories to DIY tips to gear and gun reviews, he covers it all with a perspective that’s based in experience.

#8311. No-Oxygen Team Will Try Again on Everest

Last week, high winds forced the Russian team to retreat from their third trip up the West Ridge of Everest. Sergey Kondrashkin, Vitaly Shiilov, and leader Andrei Vasiliev were climbing without oxygen, with no one else on the mountain. Winter is quickly approaching, but they have no intention of going home yet.

“We are at Lobuche…resting, analyzing the weather forecast. Still want to try again,” Vasiliev texted to ExplorersWeb over the weekend. The climbers had not intended to reach the summit on that latest attempt up the West Shoulder of Everest via the Lho La Pass. Vasiliev explained that they were not yet acclimatized enough to reach the top without bottled oxygen. They did expect to reach higher than before, but high winds made that impossible.

In a post on social media, member Sergey Kondrashkin recalls the latest trip up. On November 11, the team set off from their tent at the base of the West Ridge, fixing ropes in bitterly cold temperature. Contrary to forecasts, winds were light. The climbers pitched their tent at 6,500m and still had time to fix some ropes above them.

On the following morning, however, the gusts were too strong. After waiting in vain for the gale to abate, and with a forecast of even stronger winds the following day, Kondrashkin returned to the ridge, trying not to be blown away. He advanced between gusts and retrieved his two ice axes and the rope he had left behind the day before. They would need that gear for the descent.

Once at the bivouac, the climbers retrieved their tent, cached some food and fuel, and downclimbed to their Camp 1 at some 5,600m. It lies on the steep section between the Khumbu Glacier and the Lho La. Here, they pitched their tent again.

On Friday, the Russian team rappelled down to the glacier, on a “relatively safe” line to the right, where an avalanche fell as they descended.

Today, the team sits at their lodge in Lobuche and looks intently at weather forecasts. “We have not yet decided when to return to the mountain,” Vasiliev told Explorersweb. “It’s all about the weather, and the forecasts announce high winds on November 20-21.”

#8310. How to Use a Stalker Decoy for Taking Bucks at Close Range

Published Nov 10, 2026 4:25 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Four long years were spent eating tag soup during my annual archery trip to the prairie in search of whitetail and mule deer. In the vast flatland, stalking with nothing more than ankle-high grass as cover proved to be more of a fool’s errand than a difficult hunting strategy. Getting to 100 yards was easy, but closing the final 50 to 60 yards was nearly impossible. Eventually, I had reached a point where enough was enough, and I began searching for a tool to help me close the distance.

That’s when I came across the Ultimate Predator Stalker Decoy. This bow-mounted decoy attaches easily and is just realistic enough to trick a rutted-up buck in the middle of November. Since I began using the decoy on my annual trip, I’ve tagged out every year.

​While that sounds fine and dandy as a brief overview, I’d be lying if I said it’s been easy to fill those tags. On rare occasions, the decoy helps finish the stalk for me and puts an unwary whitetail right in my lap. Most of the time, however, my stalks end the same way they did before I had the decoy, with the deer spooking and running into the next county.

On those adrenaline-pumping occasions that it works, it’s worth the countless heartbreaks leading up to that moment. Over the years, I’ve learned a few rules that can’t be broken if you want to be successful. Below are the five rules for reaping whitetail deer that you need to know.

Just like stalking in deep ravines or picking out a treestand location, factoring in the wind and thermals is a necessity when reaping whitetail deer. In my experience, there’s not a single product on the market that you can apply or wear that will beat a deer’s sense of smell when they’re directly downwind. Since you’re reaping from the ground, there’s nothing you can do other than to approach from an angle where your wind and thermals won’t be a factor.

When reaping deer, they’ll usually try to circle downwind. This is a natural movement pattern that is hardwired and unbreakable. Knowing this, you have to use the terrain and landmark features, like a thick treeline or fence row, to your advantage.

Once a buck locks on to you, it’s not going to want to lose sight of you when approaching. If you can get near a fence row that is perpendicular to the wind direction and in range, you’re increasing your odds of getting a shot off.

Aside from the wind, which applies to all forms of deer hunting, this rule is the most important rule of them all. If you’re trying to reap a deer in the big woods of the Northeast, or the prairie out West, a buck in lockdown is going to be the most willing to march your way.

When a buck is in lockdown with a doe in estrus, he’s arguably at the peak of his aggression towards other bucks. In the multiple days of lockdown, it’s likely that he will fend off multiple different bucks trying to gain access to the doe. This natural instinct works in your favor when reaping.​

If you’re able to approach a lockdown buck with the wind in your favor, he’ll likely be willing to close the distance for you. That’s not always a guarantee, as factors like the doe running off or the buck not feeling aggressive in that moment may come into play, but lockdown is the scenario I crave when reaping whitetail deer.

But if you use the terrain to your advantage and close the gap to 80 yards and then appear with the wind in your favor, your odds of triggering an aggressive reaction go up in a hurry. This is even more important in a lockdown scenario. Getting in close to the buck and doe prior to being seen and then popping out almost in bow range can force a rutting buck to pick a fight over flight just based on his instincts kicking in.

In a lockdown scenario, your biggest threat of getting busted while reaping is always the doe. Unlike her male counterpart, she doesn’t have insanely high doses of testosterone that make her more susceptible to letting her guard down. Typically, she’s always on high alert.

That means your approach with the decoy has to be very well thought out. Rather than just bullrushing in and trying to pop the bubble by any means necessary, a more subtle approach is to pop the bubble in a way where only the buck can see you. In most cases, the buck will bed within 15 to 20 yards of the doe. In the best-case scenario, this leaves you with a tight window to approach with the wind in your favor and pop the bubble without being visible to the doe. It’s not always possible, but I’m referring to the ideal setup here.

By approaching the buck and leaving the doe oblivious to your presence, you’re much more likely to elicit an aggressive reaction from the buck without the doe spooking and taking him with her. In the end, you must remember that no matter how realistic your decoy looks, the buck cares more about the doe than you and will skip out on a fight to stick with his doe without a moment’s notice.

This final rule has been the difference maker for me on three of my four successful reapings. Whether you’re using a grunt tube to create low tending or loud challenging grunts, snort wheezing to elicit a reaction, or even just stomping the ground once you’ve popped the bubble, it’s pivotal that you act like a buck when reaping.

A challenging buck doesn’t just meander in a straight line and walk right up to a lockdown buck and doe. They stomp, they grunt, and they slowly circle in preparation for a fight. Watch any video of bucks fighting, and pay close attention to the moments before the fight. Their body posture and positioning can cue you in on minute details to replicate when reaping.

​I’ve found most success in a stomp and snort wheeze combination. This typically convinces the buck that I’m there to challenge him for his doe, and that’s when he bristles up, pins his ears back, and begins marching in my direction. Once I see those telltale signs, my focus switches to my rangefinder and release.

This tactic isn’t very popular or mainstream. That’s for good reason. The success rate is admittedly low. For every successful reaping, there are 20 to 30 unsuccessful approaches in the rearview mirror. You have to be willing to fail time and time again in order to have one successful hunt.

I would also be remiss to not mention that safety is paramount when reaping. Do not, under any circumstances, reap on public land during a firearms season. Even in archery, deploying hunter’s orange on the decoy and your person would be a strong recommendation of mine. No deer is worth dying over.​

With that said, reaping whitetail deer is something I look forward to attempting every year. It’s a wonderful way to break up the monotony of sitting in a treestand for hours on end. It’s physically and mentally challenging, and has led to some of the most exciting moments in my hunting career. There’s nothing better than tricking a mature buck into pinning his ears back and approaching you, ready for a fight.

Derek Horner is the manager of audience development for Outdoor Life where he manages OL’s social accounts and email campaigns, and shares our stories with the world. He was born and raised in central Pennsylvania where his father and grandfather taught him to hunt and fish. He graduated from Pennsylvania State University in 2018 and still lives in central Pennsylvania.

#8306. ‘The Safety Was On!’ I Nearly Missed My Chance at a Big Adirondack Buck

Published Nov 11, 2026 6:45 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

“Ho!” said Hugh, and he beat me to it by a split second. I had already puckered my lips to whistle. Hugh Fosburgh, who knows as much about whitetail deer as any man I know, had marked the little patch of sunlit runway as the place to stop the deer. The buck performed as though he had been trained in an obedience school. He stopped, keeping his nose to the trail for a long moment before he lifted his grizzled head and turned it slowly in our direction.

A moment before, he had been a shadowy shape moving through the brush; now he was a picturebook buck. Slanting rays of cold November sunlight picked out every point on his big rack and colored them a rich antique ivory.

I had first seen the buck 47 minutes before, and in those minutes the big deer had acted out a whole literature of whitetail lore.

When Hugh stopped the buck, I was standing near a hardwood log in a little staging area used by pulp cutters, and my thought was: It can’t be. It can’t be! I was not accustomed to that kind of hunter’s luck, and if the truth is to be told, I didn’t deserve it.

For a moment I thought I would kneel to shoot, but a little clump of beech brush wiped out that notion. I had raised the rifle with a kind of brainless calm, put the tapered post on his lung area, and squeezed the trigger. That trigger had always broken as cleanly as a glass swizzle stick, but now it resisted. I didn’t realize how excited I had become until I found myself jerking crazily on the trigger. A curtain of dark woods dropped into the sight picture, and the post was wavering and drifting. The safety was on!

In extenuation I must confess that in the previous 47 minutes I must have pressed the trigger-guard safety of the Remington 760 off and on at least seven times, and when I finally got a clear shot, I must have pressed it wrong.

“Well, you had cause to be nervous,” Hugh said later. “I know all about that kind of thing. Remember, I missed one at 50 yards, standing, just two days before you arrived.”

Hugh had told me that story at breakfast the day I got to camp. I had met him in a Manhattan restaurant the previous August. When he discovered that I was a fellow hunter, he said, “You must come up and hunt a few days at the camp.” I needed no urging, and after a nine-mile ride over a rutty dirt-and-gravel track from Minerva, New York, through deep forest, we emerged in a large clearing. I discovered I was visiting the 5,500-acre North Woods Club.

Hugh gave me a handsome printed map of that marvelous wilderness in the Adirondacks, and a legend told me that Winslow Homer, the American artist, had been a charter member and that all of his famous Adirondack paintings had been done there. There on the map were Mink Pond, Beaver Mountain, and the Outlet. Three Homer masterpieces—The Smudge Fire, The Young Huntsman, and The Blue Boat—loomed up in my mind’s eye.

The first day, we drove Hugh’s four-wheel-drive to where he had missed his buck. Two does and two fawns came out into full view, and a buck ran into the swamp behind a little rise of ground. I saw his antlers at the top of each bound, but that was all.

On the third day I hunted alone in the morning and had a fine experience with a big doe that walked daintily to within 12 yards of me as I sat on a fallen log above a marsh.

That afternoon Hugh said he would drive me over woods roads to give me a better look at the country. Then he would put me on stand to hunt until dark. We drove around to look at the views that Winslow Homer had painted, looked at two cubby-pen fisher sets (a trap arrangement involving a baited shelter arranged near a watercourse), visited the highest beaver dam I had ever seen, and drove a woods road onto a steep shoulder of Beaver Mountain. We got out and walked down toward some scraggly second-growth bottoms.

“I think we’ll just sit here for a while,” Hugh said. “That little flat is a good deer crossing.”

The sun had broken through the heavy overcast, and we were grateful for its quick warmth. We chatted in low tones. The dry humor sprinkled through Hugh’s low-voiced comments was diverting, but I did hear the sharp snap of a dry branch. It was the kind of noise a heavy animal makes.

“There’s a deer,” Hugh said perfunctorily, as if seeing a deer was a routine matter. I saw the deer at the same time, and it was no routine deer. I couldn’t see antlers, but even in the brush at about 140 yards, the animal’s body was burly and male. In two quick steps the deer vanished. It was exactly 2:45; I looked at my watch and recorded the time and many other things in my journal that night.

While the deer was out of sight, I swung my legs around the butt of the log so I could face in the right direction. I did it slowly, and I remember thinking: Hey, my right leg hasn’t started to tremble yet. My right knee always knocks all by itself whenever I see or hear game, especially deer.

The buck was puttering around like a cranky old bachelor doing chores. First one piece of brush moved, then another swayed. A whole mass of brush moved violently in a jerky but rhythmic fashion. I sweated out four to five minutes of this. I must have started to rise, for Hugh whispered, “Take it easy; he’ll show himself.”

He did, but only for a second. I saw two legs, then two more from the knees down as he passed a low opening in the cover.

I strained my eyes to pick him out through the half-light that filtered down through the scattering of big and small pines, beech brush, and hazel bushes. He seemed to be moving to our left.

I pushed off the pumpgun’s safety and held on a narrow opening, but the buck turned before he reached it and angled toward us. He was still 100 yards away, but I was alarmed because Hugh and I were just high enough above the flat so that the rim would soon blot out the deer.

Time went by, and then Hugh jabbed a finger at a spot much farther to the left.

“It’s a buck—and a good one,” he said. Then I saw the head. The animal raised it just once and quickly lowered it, but the glimpse of the huge antlers was enough. My right knee began to perform.

All this while I was raising and lowering the rifle to squint through the 22X Weaver scope, but it was useless. Then the deer walked right out into the open and stood quite still. When I swung the rifle to cover him, I saw that “he” had no antlers—it was a big doe! The buck was still hidden. This quite unnerved me, and my second symptom of buck fever hit. My arms seemed suddenly drained of all strength and took on that sickly spaghettilike feeling. I managed to whisper, “My God, another one!” Just then the buck walked through the same narrow opening and was gone. Miraculously, he seemed to walk through the opening again, going in the same direction. I was flabbergasted but it was a second doe.

Our problem was complicated by a factor of three. There were three deer in the brushy tangle, and while I was trying to find the needle in the haystack, I must have had every view of a deer that a man can see in the woods—a patch of brown, a glimmer of white, a dark-gray fleeting shadow, a leg, a flicking ear, a backline moving with measured stride and then hurrying across an opening in the thick second growth. Here a motion, there a contour. It went on and on while the three deer went through their annual rutting ritual. The does were playing hard to get, but the buck was persistent, and I hoped against hope that one of the does would submit and that the buck would rise above the brush as he mounted her.

And it did happen. In a rising lunge, the buck’s body heaved above the cover as he mounted a doe. Head, antlers, bulging neck, and shoulders were all in sight. I could have fired had I not hesitated for a moment to appreciate the startling sight. As I rose, the doe evidently slid and skittered out from under the eager buck because he dropped from sight. I could hear the deer, but I could no longer see them. They were cracking twigs and rustling dry bushes. The swaying bushes showed that the buck was now chasing one or both does.

Once or twice Hugh pointed, but finally, even from his slightly different angle, he could see only the swaying bushes. The wind was blowing directly across our front, and I knew our scent would soon alarm the deer because they were angling to our left and coming much closer. Soon the animals would be under the bank that dropped off below us and directly downwind as well. Then there was silence—nothing. I felt the breeze on my right cheek.

Hugh rose slowly and motioned me forward to the edge of the dropoff. By pointing to himself and gesturing, he indicated that he would circle around to our left to see if he could move the deer back past me. I was doubtful; if he moved that way, I thought he would give them his wind. But I slipped toward the dropoff.

Hugh noiselessly edged to our left, and then one and another of the deer blew. The ragged exhale of breath of the second snort ended in an angry, screeching whistle. The deer were close under the dropoff, and they went out in wild confusion. One doe bounded off in the open to my right. Then I saw the flag of another deer going away. Buck or doe, I could not tell.

Hugh came back chagrined because he had spooked them.

“Did you see him?” he asked. I shook my head. “I thought not,” he whispered. “I think the buck ran off to our left. I think he’s still in there.”

Figuring nothing ventured, nothing gained, I gave the long, rasping gutteral Brrkkkk sound that rutting bucks sometimes make. Instantly the does blew almost in unison far up the little valley.

“They didn’t like what they heard,” Hugh said ruefully.

I waited two or three minutes and then made the Brrrrrkkkk sound again, hoping our lovesick quarry would think he had a rival nearby and challenge him. Hugh took a dim view of my calling.

“Let’s smoke another cigaret and then go back to the Jeep,” he told me. “Then I’ll drive you out on that woods road and put you on stand for the evening.”

It was 3:30 when we settled down again, chatting in low tones and listening to the slow yank, yank of a nuthatch. Far up the narrow part of the little valley where the two does had gone, I heard the long, drawn-out churr and bark of a short-tempered red squirrel. I glanced in that direction and caught a motion in the brush. It materialized into a deer walking precisely through the little glade where we had first seen the buck. It was another doe.

“There’s another,” Hugh said and pointed. Sauntering behind the first one was a second doe, and just behind her I saw a third deer—a six-point buck. He took a little hop or two, caught up with the doe, and, with his nose nudging her tail, followed her.

“The woods are full of deer,” I murmured.

Hugh said, “Get up on the road if you want that six-pointer.” He was saying it when a yearling buck came along behind the first buck. I had risen and was starting toward the woods road when the hatrack buck that had shaken me earlier stepped into the clearing behind the yearling. His head was down, but he was in full view, walking rapidly in the wake of the four deer ahead of him.

As he went out of the scope’s circle behind the cover, I swung ahead and held on another sunlit clearing. I was getting set to whistle to stop him when he came into view, but Hugh whispered, “I’ll stop him when he gets into the clearing.”

“Ho!” said Hugh, just as the buck came into full view at what I later determined was 140 yards. At the sound the buck stopped dead, and raised and turned his head toward us. I saw the ivory tips of his broad antlers, but he seemed a long way off. Curiously, I was suddenly calm. The post covered his lung area, and I began the squeeze—with the crazy results already described.

When I discovered that the safety was on, I lost my wits. My arms seemed weak again, and my right knee bounced wildly. The post wandered all over the deer after I finally pushed the safety off. When the rifle roared, I knew the hold was too far back.

The rifle went up in recoil, but then I saw the buck swinging away from me. I pumped another .30/06 cartridge into the 760, but the buck was gone.

“You hit him,” Hugh said matter-of-factly. I was amazed.

“If I did, I don’t know where. The post was all over him.” I felt a mounting disgust with myself. It had been 14 years and seven big-game animals since I had failed to kill with one hit.

“You hit him hard,” Hugh told me. “I saw him flinch. Didn’t you see how heavily he moved?”

Hugh was right. The deer had not gone off in big, high bounds. “Maybe—maybe,” I ventured.

“Oh, I’m sure of it,” Hugh remarked with an easy grin.

“We ought to wait a while and let him stiffen up,” I said. “He may be hit too far back—if he is hit. That was pretty poor shooting.”

“We’ll smoke another cigaret, and then see,” Hugh replied.

After 10 or 15 minutes Hugh said, “I’ll spot you from here. Go down and see what happened.” I thought it a bit early and decided to go only as far as the spot where the buck had stood when I’d fired.

When I got there I found nothing. I walked a bit farther in spite of myself and then went another five yards. I found a tuft of hair.

But it was pure white hair. My heart sank. “A belly shot, low down,” I said to myself.

I moved alongside the scuffed leaves, and then I saw a 10-inch patch of blood. But it didn’t look like lung blood. Too dark.

With a heavy heart I went back to where the deer had stood and waved Hugh toward me. When he walked up, I pointed out the hair, and Hugh picked it up.

“He’s hit too low, but there’s blood ahead,” I told him.

Hugh followed me to the first patch of blood, reached down in the little hollow where the blood was already coagulating, and picked up a piece of tissue.

“This is a piece of lung,” he said. And it did look like it and feel like it, but I was dubious.

“Let’s work ahead slowly,” Hugh suggested. We did, and found a few red drops here and there. One of us stood at the last drop until the other located the next. We were being very quiet. Twice we found puddles of blood. At one, we could see that the buck had lain down or had fallen down. I also noticed that the wounded buck had stirred up the leaves in a continuous disturbed line by dragging his feet.

We had come perhaps 150 yards on the blood trail when we heard a rustling and thrashing in front of us and to the left. Then it stopped. We listened but heard nothing more until a nuthatch suddenly gave its wild alarm cries.

We stopped beside a big boulder a few feet from a fallen yellow birch. In getting over it the buck had left a smudge of blood. We stood still and listened again.

“Let’s be patient,” Hugh said. “He didn’t get up, I’m sure, and with the vitality deer have during the rut, I’d just as soon he didn’t get on his feet again.”

I was more than agreeable. Frankly, I thought we had followed too far and too soon.

We sat in silence on the boulder for 10 more minutes and then worked ahead a few yards. Peering intently into the dark woods, I finally spotted him through my scope or thought I did.

Read Next: Forget Trail Cameras and Feeders. Try Hunting Wilderness Whitetails the Old Way

“He’s right there,” Hugh said with certainty. And the deer never moved. He was dead, probably from loss of blood.

The buck was a fine, big, swollen-necked nine-pointer, and Hugh held out a big hand for a congratulatory shake. The buck had been hit high in the ham, and the exit wound was in the flank on the other side. The paunch was broken. Certainly the tissue Hugh and I had taken for lung was not. But the buck was ours, and I realized again how important it is to follow up whenever you fire at game.

During the dressing out and the drag, I kept thinking of Winslow Homer’s painting, The Young Huntsman. I could see the youthful hunter and a big buck’s head partly rolled up in the deerskin. It was a strange feeling to live again a scene that had been painted many years before.

This story was originally published in the November 1976 issue of Outdoor Life. Angus Cameron, the author, was 68 years old at the time of publication. He was an editor for Alfred A. Knopf, the prestigious New York City book publisher.
